Projection algebras and free projection- and idempotent-generated regular ∗ * -semigroups


Abstract
The purpose of this paper is to introduce a new family of semigroups—the free projection-generated regular ∗ * -semigroups—and initiate their systematic study. Such a semigroup PG ⁡ ( P ) \operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P) is constructed from a projection algebra P P , using the recent groupoid approach to regular ∗ * -semigroups. The assignment P ↦ PG ⁡ ( P ) P\mapsto\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P) is a left adjoint to the forgetful functor that maps a regular ∗ * -semigroup S S to its projection algebra 𝐏 ⁡ ( S ) {\bf P}(S) . In fact, the category of projection algebras is coreflective in the category of regular ∗ * -semigroups. The algebra 𝐏 ⁡ ( S ) {\bf P}(S) uniquely determines the biordered structure of the idempotents 𝐄 ⁡ ( S ) {\bf E}(S) , up to isomorphism, and this leads to a category equivalence between projection algebras and regular ∗ * -biordered sets. As a consequence, PG ⁡ ( P ) \operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P) can be viewed as a quotient of the classical free idempotent-generated (regular) semigroups IG ⁡ ( E ) \operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E) and RIG ⁡ ( E ) \operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E) , where E = 𝐄 ⁡ ( PG ⁡ ( P ) ) E={\bf E}(\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)) ; this is witnessed by a number of presentations in terms of generators and defining relations. The semigroup PG ⁡ ( P ) \operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P) can also be interpreted topologically, through a natural link to the fundamental groupoid of a simplicial complex explicitly constructed from P P . The above theory is illustrated on a number of examples. In one direction, the free construction applied to the projection algebras of adjacency semigroups yields a new family of graph-based path semigroups. In another, it turns out that, remarkably, the Temperley–Lieb monoid 𝒯 ​ ℒ n \mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n} is the free regular ∗ * -semigroup over its own projection algebra 𝐏 ⁡ ( 𝒯 ​ ℒ n ) {\bf P}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}) . Keywords : Regular ∗ * -semigroup, projection algebra, chained projection groupoid, free projection-generated regular ∗ * -semigroup, forgetful functor, adjoint, coreflectivity, regular semigroup, biordered set, free idempotent-generated semigroup, presentation, fundamental groupoid, Temperley–Lieb monoid. MSC: 20M50 (primary), 18B40, 20M05, 20M10, 20M17, 20M20.

1
Introduction

Set-based free objects exist for many classes of algebras, such as groups, monoids, lattices, rings and modules. These are typically built from a base set, and defined in terms of a universal mapping property. Formally, the existence of such free algebras in a category
{\bf C}
amounts to the forgetful functor
{\bf C}\to{\bf Set}
(which maps an algebra to its underlying set) having a left adjoint
{\bf Set}\to{\bf C}
; full definitions will be recalled later in the paper. Such an adjoint exists for example if
{\bf C}
forms a variety
[
7
,
6
,
11
]
, but this is not the case in general, even for some ‘classical’ algebras such as fields
[
6
, Exercise 2.3]
.
From the early days of semigroup theory, it was recognised that the idempotents form a useful ‘skeleton’ of a semigroup. As the theory developed, it emerged that this phenomenon was governed by the existence of forgetful functors from various classes of semigroups into categories of idempotent-like structures such as semilattices, biordered sets and others. Adjoints of some of these functors led to important classes of free semigroups, which will be discussed more below.

The current paper is concerned with
regular
*
-semigroups
. These were introduced in
[
55
]
as an intermediate class between inverse semigroups and regular semigroups. They have attained prominence recently, as the so-called diagram monoids come equipped with a natural regular
*
-structure
[
28
,
48
,
27
,
4
,
3
,
8
]
. These monoids are the building blocks of diagram algebras, such as the Brauer, Temperley–Lieb and partition algebras, among others, which in turn have important applications in theoretical physics, low-dimensional topology, representation theory, and many other parts of mathematics and science
[
35
,
9
,
57
,
47
,
42
,
43
]
.
Every regular
*
-semigroup
S
contains a set
{\bf P}(S)
of distinguished idempotents known as projections, which can be given the structure of a
projection algebra

[
39
]
. The category
{\bf PA}
of such algebras played a key role in the groupoid representation of regular
*
-semigroups in the recent paper
[
26
]
. The assignment
S\mapsto{\bf P}(S)
is a forgetful functor from the category
{\bf RSS}
of regular
*
-semigroups to
{\bf PA}
.
It turns out that this functor has a left adjoint
{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSS}
, and this leads to the notion of a
free (projection-generated) regular
*
-semigroup

\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
over a projection algebra
P
. The purpose of this paper is to show how to construct these free semigroups, and initiate their systematic study.

The link between regular
*
-semigroups and their projection algebras has many parallels with the link between arbitrary semigroups and their biordered sets of idempotents; the latter form the cornerstone of Nambooripad’s theory of regular semigroups
[
51
]
. In that situation we have a forgetful functor
{\bf E}:{\bf Sgp}\to{\bf BS}
, which maps a semigroup
S
to its biordered set

E={\bf E}(S)=\{e\in S:e^{2}=e\}.

The latter has the structure of a partial algebra, where a product
ef
(for
e,f\in E
) is only defined if at least one of
ef
or
fe
is equal to
e
or
f
.
A deep result of Easdown and Nambooripad states that
{\bf E}
has a left adjoint
{\bf BS}\to{\bf Sgp}
. This formulation can be found in
[
52
, Theorem 6.10]
, but has its basis in
[
24
, Theorem 3.3]
.
The adjoint of
{\bf E}
maps an (abstract) biordered set
E
to the
free (idempotent-generated) semigroup

\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
, which is defined by the presentation

\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)=\langle X_{E}:x_{e}x_{f}=x_{ef}\ \text{if $ef$ is defined in $E$}\rangle,

where here
X_{E}=\{x_{e}:e\in E\}
is an alphabet in one-one correspondence with
E
. The key point is that the biordered set of the semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
is precisely
E
, when one identifies
e\in E
with the equivalence class of the one-letter word
x_{e}
.

The biorder approach has additional power in the case of regular semigroups. The restriction of the above forgetful functor
{\bf E}:{\bf Sgp}\to{\bf BS}
to regular semigroups maps into the category of regular biordered sets, which were axiomatised by Nambooripad
[
51
]
, but the adjoint
{\bf BS}\to{\bf Sgp}
maps regular biordered sets to non-regular semigroups in general. Instead, the restriction has a different adjoint, mapping
E
to the
free regular (idempotent-generated) semigroup

\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)
; see
[
51
]
for a combinatorial/topological definition, and
[
56
]
for a presentation.

The free semigroups
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
and
\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)
turn out to have very intricate structure, and have therefore become a subject of broad interest in their own right
[
51
,
53
,
24
,
33
,
34
,
22
,
21
,
17
,
15
,
10
,
56
,
49
,
19
,
16
,
18
,
32
,
23
]
.
As one strand of research, it was shown in
[
10
]
that maximal subgroups of
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
and
\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)
are (isomorphic to) fundamental groups of certain natural complexes associated to
E
. The main motivation for this result was to address a folklore conjecture that such maximal subgroups were always free, and the topological viewpoint led to the discovery of a biordered set inducing the non-free subgroup
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
; see
[
10
, Section 5]
. Soon after, the conjecture was turned upside down, when it was shown in
[
33
]
that
every
group is isomorphic to a maximal subgroup of some
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
. Since then, many substantial studies have emerged exploring the structure of
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
and
\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)
in the case that
E={\bf E}(S)
is the biordered set of some important semigroup
S

[
34
,
21
,
15
,
19
]
.

We note in passing that biordered sets are not the only structures that have been used to model the idempotent ‘skeleton’ of semigroups. For example, early work focussed on the so-called
warp
of a semigroup
S

[
56
,
12
,
13
,
59
]
, which was again a partial algebra with underlying set
E={\bf E}(S)
, but which retained
all
products
ef
for which
e,f,ef\in E
. Similarly, the biordered set can sometimes be given additional structure, as is indeed the case with regular
*
-semigroups, whose biordered sets have involutions
[
54
]
.

We now return to our main topic, regular
*
-semigroups and projection algebras, linked by the forgetful functor
{\bf P}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}
. The latter maps a regular
*
-semigroup
S
to its set

P={\bf P}(S)=\{p\in S:p^{2}=p=p^{*}\}

of
projections
, which is then given the structure of a
projection algebra
, as originally defined (under a different name) by Imaoka
[
39
]
. This algebra has a unary operation
\theta_{p}
for each
p\in P
, which is defined by
q\theta_{p}=pqp
for
q\in P
. Such algebras were axiomatised in
[
39
]
, where it was shown that they are the appropriate vehicle for transformation representations of
fundamental
regular
*
-semigroups, building on work of Munn in the inverse case
[
50
]
; see also
[
41
]
.

Projection algebras took on a new level of significance in
[
26
]
, where they became the (structured) object sets of so-called
chained projection groupoids
. The main result
[
26
, Theorem 8.1]
is a category isomorphism
{\bf RSS}\cong{\bf CPG}
, where the latter is the category of all such groupoids. These groupoids are in fact triples
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
, where
P
is an (abstract) projection algebra,
\mathcal{G}
is an ordered groupoid whose structure has tight algebraic and order-theoretic links to
P
, and
\varepsilon:\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{G}
is a functor from a natural
chain groupoid

\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)
built from
P
. Seen through the groupoid lens, the forgetful functor
{\bf CPG}\to{\bf PA}
maps
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)\mapsto P
. The key construction in the current paper is a left adjoint
{\bf PA}\to{\bf CPG}
. This maps
P\mapsto(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)
, where
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is an appropriate quotient of
\mathscr{C}
, and
\nu:\mathscr{C}\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is the quotient map. Topologically,
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is the fundamental groupoid of a natural complex built from
P
.
Applying the isomorphism
{\bf S}:{\bf CPG}\to{\bf RSS}
from
[
26
]
yields an adjoint
{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSS}
to the forgetful functor
{\bf P}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}
, and hence establishes the existence of the
free (projection-generated) regular
*
-semigroups

\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)={\bf S}(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)
.
In fact, we show that the adjoint is a right inverse of
{\bf P}
, and this has the consequence that
{\bf PA}
is
coreflective
in
{\bf RSS}
(and in
{\bf CPG}
).
We also remark that the existence of the adjoint answers a question not settled by the isomorphism
{\bf RSS}\cong{\bf CPG}
from
[
26
]
, in that it shows that every projection algebra
P
can be realised as
P={\bf P}(S)
for some regular
*
-semigroup
S
. This fact was first established by Imaoka in his above-mentioned work on fundamental semigroups
[
39
]
; see also
[
41
]
.

At this point, the semigroups
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
become the subject of the rest of the paper. There are a number of ways to understand these semigroups, starting from their original definition as
chain semigroups

\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)={\bf S}(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)
. It is immediate from our construction that
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is generated by
P
. Building on this, another of our main results establishes a presentation

\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\cong\langle X_{P}:x_{p}^{2}=x_{p},\ (x_{p}x_{q})^{2}=x_{p}x_{q},\ x_{p}x_{q}x_{p}=x_{q\theta_{p}}\ \text{for }p,q\in P\rangle,

where
X_{P}=\{x_{p}:p\in P\}
is an alphabet in one-one correspondence with
P
. Two further presentations
allow us to understand the explicit relationship between the new regular
*
-semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
and the classical free idempotent-generated semigroups
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
and
\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)
, where
E={\bf E}(\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P))
.

As was the case with these classical semigroups, the free regular
*
-semigroups
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
are very interesting in their own right. For example, when
P={\bf P}(A_{\Gamma})
is the projection algebra of an adjacency semigroup (in the sense of Jackson and Volkov
[
40
]
), the free semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is an apparently-new graph-based path semigroup. As another example, it turns out that a finite
Temperley–Lieb monoid

\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
is a free regular
*
-semigroup over its own projection algebra
{\bf P}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})
, giving yet another fundamental way to understand this important diagram monoid. The situation for other diagram monoids is more delicate, and is taken up for the partition monoid in the forthcoming paper
[
29
]
.

We now give a brief overview of the structure of the paper; the introduction to each section contains a fuller summary of its contents.

•

Sections
2
and
3
contain the preliminary material we need. The former covers the basics on regular
*
-semigroups, and provides some key examples, namely adjacency semigroups and diagram monoids.
The latter gives an overview of the relevant constructions and results from
[
26
]
, concerning regular
*
-semigroups, projection algebras and chained projection groupoids, leading up to the isomorphism
{\bf RSS}\cong{\bf CPG}
.

•

Sections
4
and
5
are central for this paper: they introduce the semigroups
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
, and demonstrate their freeness. The former is the content of Definition
4.21
and Theorem
4.22
. The latter is achieved in Theorem
5.1
, which shows that the functor
{{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSS}:P\mapsto\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)}
is indeed a left adjoint to the forgetful functor
{{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}:S\mapsto{\bf P}(S)}
, and also establishes the coreflectivity of
{\bf PA}
in
{\bf RSS}
. A number of semigroup-theoretic consequences are given in Theorems
5.8
and
5.9
.

•

Section
6
explores the connection between projection algebras and regular
*
-biordered sets, as defined in
[
54
]
. The main result here is Theorem
6.19
, which establishes an equivalence between the categories
{\bf PA}
and
{\bf RSBS}
of all such structures. As a consequence, Theorem
6.20
shows that the semigroups
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
are also free with respect to the forgetful functor
{{\bf RSS}\to{\bf RSBS}:S\mapsto{\bf E}(S)}
. En route, we show in Proposition
6.10
that the projection algebra of a regular
*
-semigroup uniquely determines its biordered set, up to isomorphism.

•

In Section
7
we give three presentations (by generators and defining relations) for the semigroups
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
. The first, in Theorem
7.2
, involves
P
as a generating set. The other two, in Theorems
7.10
and
7.13
, utilise the generating set
E={\bf E}(\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P))
, and exhibit
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
as an explicit quotient of
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
and of
\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)
, respectively.

•

Sections
8
and
9
illustrate our theory on some important examples.
In the former, we will see that the free construction applied to an adjacency semigroup results in an apparently-new graph-based
bridging path semigroup
, which we believe is worthy of further study.
In the latter, we return to diagram monoids, showing in Theorem
9.1
that the free regular
*
-semigroup associated to the projection algebra of a finite Temperley–Lieb monoid
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
is, somewhat remarkably, isomorphic to
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
itself.

•

Finally, Section
10
provides a topological interpretation of the free regular
*
-semigroups, with Theorems
10.9
and
10.10
establishing a link with the fundamental group(oid)s of certain natural graphs and complexes associated to projection algebras. We conclude the paper by recasting our earlier examples using this topological framework, and exploring some further ones. As an intriguing consequence, the semigroup-theoretic structure of the Temperley–Lieb monoid
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
allows us to immediately deduce that the components of its associated complex are simply connected; this is not obvious, a priori, as these complexes are highly intricate.

A feature of the work presented here is that it establishes tight connections between different types of mathematical objects.
This has certainly caused some presentational and notational challenges for the authors.
In the hope of somewhat easing such challenges for the reader, we have collected the key notation in Tables
1
–
4
. The information presented there might not make much sense at this stage of reading the paper, but we hope that the reader can use the tables as a ready reference throughout.

Regular

-semigroups

2.1
Definitions and basic properties

Here we gather the background on regular
*
-semigroups that we will need in the rest of the paper. For proofs of the various assertions, see for example
[
55
,
39
,
26
]
. For more on semigroups in general we refer to
[
37
,
14
]
.

A
regular
*
-semigroup
is a semigroup
S
with a unary operation
{{}^{*}:S\to S:a\mapsto a^{*}}
satisfying

(a^{*})^{*}=a=aa^{*}a\and(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a,b\in S$.}

From the identity
a=aa^{*}a
, it is clear that a regular
*
-semigroup is (von Neumann) regular. The identities
(a^{*})^{*}=a
and
(ab)^{*}=b^{*}a^{*}
say that
∗
is an involution.

We write
{\bf RSS}
for the category of regular
*
-semigroups with
*
-morphisms, i.e. maps
{\phi:S\to S^{\prime}}
satisfying

(ab)\phi=(a\phi)(b\phi)\and(a^{*})\phi=(a\phi)^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $a,b\in S$.}

Given a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, we write

{\bf P}(S)=\{p\in S:p^{2}=p=p^{*}\}\and{\bf E}(S)=\{e\in S:e^{2}=e\}

for the sets of all
projections
and
idempotents
of
S
, respectively. Important properties of these elements include the following:

(RS1)

The projections are precisely the elements of the form
aa^{*}
, for
a\in S
.

(RS2)

The product of two projections is an idempotent, but need not be a projection.

(RS3)

Any idempotent
e
is the product of two projections, namely
e=(ee^{*})(e^{*}e)
.

(RS4)

The product of two idempotents need not be an idempotent.

(RS5)

For all
p,q\in{\bf P}(S)
we have
pqp\in{\bf P}(S)
.

(RS6)

More generally, we have
aqa^{*}\in{\bf P}(S)
for all
a\in S
and
q\in{\bf P}(S)
.

Our seventh item is an elaboration on
(RS3)
. For projections
p,q\in{\bf P}(S)
, we write
p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q
if
p=pqp
and
q=qpq
, and say that
p
and
q
are
friendly
.
It is easy to check that
ee^{*}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}e^{*}e
for any idempotent
e\in{\bf E}(S)
. Thus,
(RS3)
says that every idempotent is a product of a pair of
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related projections. As noted on
[
26
, p. 20]
, such expressions are unique:

(RS7)

pq=rs\ \Leftrightarrow\ [p=r\text{ and }q=s]
for all
(p,q),(r,s)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
.

More generally, any product of idempotents in a regular
*
-semigroup is equal to a product
p_{1}\cdots p_{k}
of projections satisfying
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}\cdots\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{k}
, though such expressions need not be unique; see
[
26
, Proposition 3.16]
.

Because of
(RS5)
, each projection
p\in P={\bf P}(S)
induces a map

\theta_{p}:P\to P\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003ptq\theta_{p}=pqp\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $q\in P$.}

(2.1)

Taking these maps as unary operations gives
P
the structure of a so-called
projection algebra
; we will discuss these more formally in Section
3
, and will use them extensively in the rest of the paper.

We now describe some examples that we will use to illustrate the ideas developed in the paper. For more examples, see
[
26
]
or
[
54
]
, but note that regular
*
-semigroups were called ‘special
*
-semigroups’ in the latter.

2.2
Adjacency semigroups

Adjacency semigroups were introduced in
[
40
]
, and were discussed as a key example in
[
26
]
. They can be viewed in several ways: as combinatorial completely
0
-simple regular
*
-semigroups, or as symmetrical square
0
-bands. Here we take the graph theoretic approach of
[
40
]
. General completely
0
-simple regular
*
-semigroups were discussed in detail in
[
26
, Section 3.3]
.

Let
\Gamma=(P,E)
be a symmetric, reflexive digraph, with vertex set
P
, and edge set
E\subseteq P\times P
. The
adjacency semigroup

A_{\Gamma}
is the regular
*
-semigroup with:

•

underlying set
A_{\Gamma}=(P\times P)\cup\{0\}
,

•

involution
0^{*}=0
and
(p,q)^{*}=(q,p)
, and

•

product
0^{2}=0=0(p,q)=(p,q)0
and
(p,q)(r,s)=\begin{cases}(p,s)&\text{if $(q,r)\in E$}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We identify a vertex
p\in P
with the pair
(p,p)\in A_{\Gamma}
. In this way, the projections and idempotents of
A_{\Gamma}
are the sets
P_{0}=P\cup\{0\}
and
E_{0}=E\cup\{0\}
, and we have
(p,q)=pq
for all
(p,q)\in E
. The projection algebra operations are given, for
p,q\in P_{0}
, by

q\theta_{p}=\begin{cases}p&\text{if $(p,q)\in E$}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(2.2)

In particular,
\theta_{0}
is the constant map with image
0
. It also follows that
{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}=E\cup\{(0,0)\}
.

2.3
Diagram monoids

A key class of examples of regular
*
-semigroups comes from the so-called
diagram monoids
. This class includes important families such as partition monoids
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, Brauer monoids
\mathcal{B}_{n}
, Temperley–Lieb monoids
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
, partial Brauer monoids
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
and Motzkin monoids
\mathcal{M}_{n}
. The elements of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
are the set partitions of
\{1,\ldots,n\}\cup\{1^{\prime},\ldots,n^{\prime}\}
. These partitions are represented and multiplied diagrammatically, as in Figure
1
; for formal definitions and an extended discussion see
[
26
]
. The involution
\alpha\mapsto\alpha^{*}
corresponds to a vertical reflection, as also shown in Figure
1
.
The elements of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
) are the partitions whose blocks have size
2
(resp.
\leq 2
), while
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
(resp.
\mathcal{M}_{n}
) consists of partitions from
\mathcal{B}_{n}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}\mathcal{B}_{n}
) that can be drawn in planar fashion within the rectangle bounded by the vertices. Thus, in Figure
1
we have
\beta\in\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{6}
and
\alpha\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{6}
.

\alpha=
\beta=
=\alpha\beta
\alpha=
=\alpha^{*}

Figure 1:
Diagrammatic representation, multiplication and involution in
\mathcal{P}_{6}
.

The Temperley–Lieb monoid
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
will be considered in Section
9
as a major example. To study it we will require the following well-known presentation by generators and relations; see
[
8
,
25
]
for proofs.

Theorem 2.3
.

The Temperley–Lieb monoid has monoid presentation

\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}\cong\langle X_{T}:R_{T}\rangle,

where
X_{T}=\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{n-1}\}
, and where
R_{T}
is the set of relations

\displaystyle t_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=t_{i}

for all
i

(T1)

\displaystyle t_{i}t_{j}

\displaystyle=t_{j}t_{i}

if
|i-j|>1

(T2)

\displaystyle t_{i}t_{j}t_{i}

\displaystyle=t_{i}

if
|i-j|=1
.

(T3)

In the above presentation, the generator
t_{i}
corresponds to the diagram

\tau_{i}=
1
i
i\!\!+\!\!1
n
.

(2.4)

3
Projection algebras and chained projection groupoids

In this section we give an overview of the constructions and results we will need from
[
26
]
, building up to the isomorphism between the categories of regular
*
-semigroups and chained projection groupoids; see Theorem
3.9
. The presentation here is necessarily streamlined; for more details, and for proofs of the various assertions, see
[
26
]
.

3.1
Preliminaries on small categories

Categories considered in the paper come in two kinds: large categories whose objects and morphisms are algebraic structures and structure-preserving mappings; and small categories, which are thought of as algebraic objects in their own right. The former are treated in the usual way; see for example
[
5
,
46
]
. The current section explains how we view the latter, following
[
26
]
.

A small category
\mathcal{C}
will be identified with its morphism set. The objects of
\mathcal{C}
are identified with the identities, the set of which is denoted
v\mathcal{C}
.
4
4

4

The choice of notation alludes to the graph-theoretic interpretation of objects and morphisms as vertices and edges in a digraph. This viewpoint will become prominent in the final section of the paper.

The domain and range maps are denoted
{\bf d},{\bf r}:\mathcal{C}\to v\mathcal{C}
, and we compose morphisms left-to-right, so that
a\circ b
is defined when
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, and then
{\bf d}(a\circ b)={\bf d}(a)
and
{\bf r}(a\circ b)={\bf r}(b)
. For
p,q\in v\mathcal{C}
we write

\mathcal{C}(p,q)=\{a\in\mathcal{C}:{\bf d}(a)=p,\ {\bf r}(a)=q\}

for the set of all morphisms
p\to q
.

A
*
-category
is a small category
\mathcal{C}
with an involution, i.e. a map
{\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}:a\mapsto a^{*}}
satisfying the following, for all
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
:

•

{\bf d}(a^{*})={\bf r}(a)
,
{\bf r}(a^{*})={\bf d}(a)
and
(a^{*})^{*}=a
.

•

If
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, then
(a\circ b)^{*}=b^{*}\circ a^{*}
.

A
groupoid
is a
*
-category for which we additionally have
a\circ a^{*}={\bf d}(a)
(and hence also
a^{*}\circ a={\bf r}(a)
) for all
a\in\mathcal{C}
.
In a groupoid, we typically write
a^{*}=a^{-1}
for
a\in\mathcal{C}
.

An
ordered
*
-category
(respectively,
ordered groupoid
) is a
*
-category (respectively, groupoid)
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a partial order
\leq
satisfying the following, for all
a,b,c,d\in\mathcal{C}
and
p\in v\mathcal{C}
:

•

If
a\leq b
, then
{\bf d}(a)\leq{\bf d}(b)
,
{\bf r}(a)\leq{\bf r}(b)
and
a^{*}\leq b^{*}
.

•

If
a\leq b
and
c\leq d
, and if
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(c)
and
{\bf r}(b)={\bf d}(d)
, then
a\circ c\leq b\circ d
.

•

For all
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
and
q\leq{\bf r}(a)
, there exist unique
u,v\leq a
with
{\bf d}(u)=p
and
{\bf r}(v)=q
. These elements are denoted
u={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a
and
v=a{\downharpoonright}_{q}
, and are called the
left
and
right restrictions
of
a
to
p
and
q
, respectively.

A
congruence
on a small category
\mathcal{C}
is an equivalence relation
\approx
on
\mathcal{C}
satisfying the following, for all
a,b,u,v\in\mathcal{C}
:

•

a\approx b\ \Rightarrow\ [{\bf d}(a)={\bf d}(b)
and
{\bf r}(a)={\bf r}(b)]
,

•

a\approx b\ \Rightarrow\ [u\circ a\approx u\circ b
and
a\circ v\approx b\circ v]
, whenever the stated compositions are defined.

For a subset
\Omega\subseteq\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}
with
{\bf d}(a)={\bf d}(b)
and
{\bf r}(a)={\bf r}(b)
for all
(a,b)\in\Omega
, we write
\Omega^{\sharp}
for the congruence on
\mathcal{C}
generated by
\Omega
.

An
ordered
*
-congruence
on an ordered
*
-category
\mathcal{C}
is a congruence
\approx
satisfying the following, for all
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
and
p\in v\mathcal{C}
:

•

a\approx b\ \Rightarrow\ a^{*}\approx b^{*}
,

•

[a\approx b
and
p\leq{\bf d}(a)]\ \Rightarrow\ {}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a\approx{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}b
.

When
{\approx}=\Omega^{\sharp}
, these two conditions can be verified by showing that they hold for all
(a,b)\in\Omega
; see
[
26
, Lemma 2.7]
.

Given an (ordered
*
-) congruence
\approx
on an (ordered
*
-) category
\mathcal{C}
, the quotient
\mathcal{C}/{\approx}
is an (ordered
*
-) category. Identifying an object
p\in v\mathcal{C}
with its
\approx
-class, we have
{v(\mathcal{C}/{\approx})=v\mathcal{C}}
.

3.2
Projection algebras

The properties of projections of regular
*
-semigroups are formalised in what are now known as projection algebras, going back to Imaoka
[
39
]
, who called them ‘
P
-groupoids’. Here we recall the definition of these algebras, and list some known results that will be used later in the paper.

A
projection algebra
is a unary algebra
P
, with set of operations
\{\theta_{p}:p\in P\}
in one-one correspondence with
P
, satisfying the following axioms, for all
p,q\in P
:

(P1)

p\theta_{p}=p
,

(P2)

\theta_{p}\theta_{p}=\theta_{p}
,

(P3)

p\theta_{q}\theta_{p}=q\theta_{p}
,

(P4)

\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}=\theta_{q\theta_{p}}
,

(P5)

\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=\theta_{p}\theta_{q}
.

The elements of a projection algebra are called
projections
. We write
{\bf PA}
for the category of projection algebras with projection algebra morphisms, defined as maps
\phi:P\to P^{\prime}
satisfying

(p\theta_{q})\phi=(p\phi)\theta_{q\phi}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $p,q\in P$.}

(3.1)

Projection algebras can also be thought of as binary algebras, with a single operation
\diamond
given by
q\diamond p=q\theta_{p}
. In this formulation, projection algebra morphisms are simply maps
\phi:P\to P^{\prime}
satisfying
(q\diamond p)\phi=(q\phi)\diamond(p\phi)
for
p,q\in P
. The binary approach was used in
[
41
]
, and compared in detail to the unary approach in
[
26
, Remark 4.2]
.

Given a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, the set of projections

P={\bf P}(S)=\{p\in S:p^{2}=p=p^{*}\}

becomes a projection algebra, with unary operations
\theta_{p}
as in (
2.1
). Conversely, any projection algebra is the projection algebra of some regular
*
-semigroup. This latter fact was proved in
[
39
,
41
,
26
]
, and it also follows from Theorem
4.22
below; see also
[
54
]
. The assignment
S\mapsto{\bf P}(S)
is the object part of a (forgetful) functor

{\bf P}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}.

(3.2)

Given a
*
-morphism
\phi:S\to S^{\prime}
, the projection algebra morphism
{\bf P}(\phi):{\bf P}(S)\to{\bf P}(S^{\prime})
is simply the restriction
{\bf P}(\phi)=\phi|_{{\bf P}(S)}
.
It is easy to check that
{\bf P}(\phi)
is indeed a morphism as defined in (
3.1
).

A projection algebra
P
has three associated relations,
\leq
,
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
, defined for
p,q\in P
by

p\leq q\ \Leftrightarrow\ p=p\theta_{q},\hskip 20.00003ptp\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q\ \Leftrightarrow\ p=q\theta_{p}\and p\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q\ \Leftrightarrow\ [p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q\text{ and }q\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}p].

(3.3)

The relation
\leq
is a partial order, and we have
p\leq q\ \Leftrightarrow\ p=r\theta_{q}
for some
r\in P
. The relation
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
is reflexive, and
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
is reflexive and symmetric; neither
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
nor
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
is transitive in general.

We now list some important properties of projection algebras proved in
[
26
, Section 4]
. Specifically, for any projection algebra
P
, and for any
p,q,r\in P
:

(PA1)

p\theta_{q}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q\theta_{p}
,

(PA2)

[p\leq q\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}r
or
p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q\leq r]\ \Rightarrow\ p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}r
,

(PA3)

p\leq q\ \Rightarrow\ p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q
,

(PA4)

p\leq q\ \Rightarrow\ \theta_{p}=\theta_{p}\theta_{q}=\theta_{q}\theta_{p}
,

(PA5)

p\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}q\ \Rightarrow\ \theta_{p}=\theta_{p}\theta_{q}\theta_{p}
.

We will also need the following simple result:

Lemma 3.4
.

For any
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},q,r\in P
we have

\theta_{q\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}}=\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}}\theta_{q}\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}\and r\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}}\theta_{q}\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}\theta_{r}=q\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}\theta_{r}.

Proof.

The first claim follows by iterating
(P4)
. The second follows by applying the first, and then
(P3)
:

r\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}}\theta_{q}\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}\theta_{r}=r\theta_{q\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}}\theta_{r}=q\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}\theta_{r}.\qed

3.3
Path categories and chain groupoids

Let
P
be a projection algebra. A
(
P
-)path
is a path in the graph of the
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-relation, i.e. a tuple

\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k})\in P^{k}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $k\geq 1$, such that $p_{1}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{2}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}\cdots\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{k}$.}

We write
{\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})=p_{1}
and
{\bf r}(\mathfrak{p})=p_{k}
. We identify each
p\in P
with the path
(p)
of length
1
.
The
path category
of
P
is the ordered
*
-category
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(P)
of all
P
-paths, with:

•

object set
v\mathscr{P}=P
,

•

composition
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\circ(p_{k},\ldots,p_{l})=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},\ldots,p_{l})
,

•

involution
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})^{\textup{rev}}=(p_{k},\ldots,p_{1})
,

•

restrictions
{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})
and
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}){\downharpoonright}_{r}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k})
, for
q\leq p_{1}
and
r\leq p_{k}
, where

q_{i}=q\theta_{p_{2}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}=q\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}\and r_{i}=r\theta_{p_{k-1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}=r\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $1\leq i\leq k$.}

(3.5)

Given a projection algebra
P
, we write
\Omega=\Omega(P)
for the set of all pairs
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\mathscr{P}\times\mathscr{P}
of the following two forms:

(
\mathsf{\Omega}
1)

\mathfrak{s}=(p,p)
and
\mathfrak{t}=(p)
, for some
p\in P
,

(
\mathsf{\Omega}
2)

\mathfrak{s}=(p,q,p)
and
\mathfrak{t}=(p)
, for some
(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
,

and we write
{\approx}=\Omega^{\sharp}
for the congruence on
\mathscr{P}
generated by
\Omega
. This is an ordered
*
-congruence, and the quotient is a groupoid, the
chain groupoid
of
P
:

\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)=\mathscr{P}/{\approx}.

The elements of
\mathscr{C}
are called
(
P
-)chains
, and we denote by
[\mathfrak{p}]
the chain containing the path
{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}}
.
The order in
\mathscr{C}
is determined by the restrictions, which are canonically inherited from
\mathscr{P}
. Specifically, for
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
we have

{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}[\mathfrak{p}]=[{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}]\and[\mathfrak{p}]{\downharpoonright}_{r}=[\mathfrak{p}{\downharpoonright}_{r}]\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $q\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})={\bf d}[\mathfrak{p}]$ and $r\leq{\bf r}(\mathfrak{p})={\bf r}[\mathfrak{p}]$.}

These are well defined because
\approx
is an
ordered
congruence.

Remark 3.6
.

Any chain
\mathfrak{c}=[\mathfrak{p}]\in\mathscr{C}
can be uniquely represented in the form
[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]
, where each
p_{i}
is distinct from
p_{i+1}
(if
i\leq k-1
) and from
p_{i+2}
(if
i\leq k-2
).
This ‘reduced form’ can be found by successively reducing
\mathfrak{p}
, using the rules

(p,p)\to(p)\ \ \text{for $p\in P$}\and(p,q,p)\to(p)\ \ \text{for $(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}$}.

One way to establish uniqueness is to show that the rewriting system
(\mathscr{P},{\to})
is (locally) confluent and Noetherian, in the sense of
[
38
]
.

For any projection algebra morphism
\phi:P\to P^{\prime}
, there is a well-defined ordered groupoid morphism

\mathscr{C}(\phi):\mathscr{C}(P)\to\mathscr{C}(P^{\prime})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\mathscr{C}(\phi)=[p_{1}\phi,\ldots,p_{k}\phi].

(3.7)

In this way,
\mathscr{C}
can be viewed as a functor from the category
{\bf PA}
of projection algebras to the category
{\bf OG}
of ordered groupoids.

3.4
Chained projection groupoids

A
weak projection groupoid
is a pair
(P,\mathcal{G})
, consisting of an ordered groupoid
\mathcal{G}
whose object set
P
has the structure of a projection algebra, and for which the restriction to
P
of the order on
\mathcal{G}

coincides with the projection algebra order
\leq
from (
3.3
). For any
a\in\mathcal{G}
, we have a pair of maps

\vartheta_{a}:{\bf d}(a)^{\downarrow}\to{\bf r}(a)^{\downarrow}:p\mapsto{\bf r}({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a)\and\Theta_{a}=\theta_{{\bf d}(a)}\vartheta_{a}:P\to{\bf r}(a)^{\downarrow},

where here
q^{\downarrow}=\{p\in P:p\leq q\}
is the down-set of
q\in P
.
A
projection groupoid
is a weak projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G})
for which:

(G1)

\theta_{p\Theta_{a}}=\Theta_{a^{-1}}\theta_{p}\Theta_{a}
for all
p\in P
and
a\in\mathcal{G}
.

Let
(P,\mathcal{G})
be a projection groupoid. An
evaluation map
is an ordered
v
-functor
\varepsilon:\mathscr{C}(P)\to\mathcal{G}
, meaning that the following hold:

\varepsilon(p)=p\text{ for $p\in P$},\hskip 10.00002pt\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c}\circ\mathfrak{d})=\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c})\circ\varepsilon(\mathfrak{d})\text{ if ${\bf r}(\mathfrak{c})={\bf d}(\mathfrak{d})$}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\varepsilon({}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{c})={}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}(\varepsilon(\mathfrak{c}))\text{ for $p\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{c})$.}

We note in passing that evaluation maps are written to the left of their arguments, as we feel they are easier to read this way (and never need to be composed).
Since
\mathscr{C}(P)
is generated by chains of length
2
, the functor
\varepsilon
is completely determined by the elements
\varepsilon[p,q]
for
(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
. These elements feature in the remaining assumptions and constructions involving evaluation maps.

Given a morphism
b\in\mathcal{G}
, a pair of projections
(e,f)\in P\times P
is said to be
b
-linked
if

f=e\Theta_{b}\theta_{f}\and e=f\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{e}.

Given such a
b
-linked pair
(e,f)
, and writing
q={\bf d}(b)
and
r={\bf r}(b)
, we define further projections

e_{1}=e\theta_{q},\hskip 20.00003pte_{2}=f\Theta_{b^{-1}},\hskip 20.00003ptf_{1}=e\Theta_{b}\and f_{2}=f\theta_{r}.

The groupoid
\mathcal{G}
then contains two well-defined morphisms:

\displaystyle\lambda(e,b,f)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[e,e_{1}]\circ{}_{e_{1}}{\downharpoonleft}b\circ\varepsilon[f_{1},f]

and

\displaystyle\rho(e,b,f)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[e,e_{2}]\circ{}_{e_{2}}{\downharpoonleft}b\circ\varepsilon[f_{2},f]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[e,e_{1}]\circ b{\downharpoonright}_{f_{1}}\circ\varepsilon[f_{1},f]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[e,e_{2}]\circ b{\downharpoonright}_{f_{2}}\circ\varepsilon[f_{2},f].

A
chained projection groupoid
is a triple
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
, where
(P,\mathcal{G})
is a projection groupoid, and
\varepsilon:\mathscr{C}(P)\to\mathcal{G}
is an evaluation map for which:

(G2)

\lambda(e,b,f)=\rho(e,b,f)
for every
b\in\mathcal{G}
, and every
b
-linked pair
(e,f)
.

We write
{\bf CPG}
for the category of all chained projection groupoids with
chained projection functors
as morphisms. A chained projection functor
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)\to(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})
is an ordered groupoid functor
\phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
such that

•

v\phi=\phi|_{P}:P\to P^{\prime}
is a projection algebra morphism, and

•

\phi
respects evaluation maps, in the sense that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(P)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(P^{\prime})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{\prime},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(v\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

where
\mathscr{C}(v\phi)
is constructed from
v\phi
as in (
3.7
). Explicitly, this is to say that

(\varepsilon[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}])\phi=\varepsilon^{\prime}[p_{1}\phi,\ldots,p_{k}\phi]

whenever
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\in P
and
p_{1}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}\cdots\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{k}
.

3.5
A category isomorphism

The main result of
[
26
]
is that the categories
{\bf RSS}
and
{\bf CPG}
, of regular
*
-semigroups and chained projection groupoids, are isomorphic. The proof involves two functors,
{\bf G}
and
{\bf S}
, between the two categories, which operate as follows.

A regular
*
-semigroup
S
determines a chained projection groupoid
{\bf G}(S)=(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
, where:

•

P
is the projection algebra of
S
.

•

\mathcal{G}
is an ordered groupoid built from
S
as follows. The morphisms are the elements of
S
, and the objects/identities are the projections, with
{\bf d}(a)=aa^{*}
and
{\bf r}(a)=a^{*}a
for
a\in S
. The composition and involution are given by
a\circ b=ab
when
{\bf r}(a)={\bf d}(b)
, and
a^{-1}=a^{*}
. Restrictions are given by
{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}a=pa
and
a{\downharpoonright}_{q}=aq
for
p\leq{\bf d}(a)
and
q\leq{\bf r}(a)
.

•

\varepsilon:\mathscr{C}(P)\to\mathcal{G}
is the evaluation map given by
\varepsilon[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]=p_{1}\cdots p_{k}
, where this product is taken in
S
.

Any
*
-morphism
S\to S^{\prime}
is also a chained projection functor
{\bf G}(S)\to{\bf G}(S^{\prime})
.

Conversely, any chained projection groupoid
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
gives rise to a regular
*
-semigroup
{\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
, with underlying set
\mathcal{G}
, and:

•

involution given by
a^{*}=a^{-1}
,

•

product defined, for
a,b\in\mathcal{G}
with
{\bf r}(a)=p
and
{\bf d}(b)=q
, by

a\bullet b=a{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}\circ\varepsilon[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]\circ{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}b,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptp^{\prime}=q\theta_{p}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptq^{\prime}=p\theta_{q}.

(3.8)

Any chained projection functor
(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)\to(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})
is also a
*
-morphism
{{\bf S}(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)\to{\bf S}(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})}
.

Theorem 3.9

(see
[
26
, Theorem 8.1]
)
.

{\bf G}
and
{\bf S}
are mutually inverse isomorphisms between the categories
{\bf RSS}
and
{\bf CPG}
. ∎

4
Construction of the chain semigroup

We now come to the main focus of our study: the chain semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
associated to a projection algebra
P
. This semigroup is built in Subsection
4.3
by first constructing a chained projection groupoid
(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)
, and then applying the functor
{{\bf S}:{\bf CPG}\to{\bf RSS}}
from Theorem
3.9
. Here
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is a homomorphic image of the chain groupoid
\mathscr{C}
(see Subsection
4.2
), and
\nu
is the quotient map
\mathscr{C}\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}
. The definition of
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
involves the notion of
linked pairs
of projections, which are introduced in Subsection
4.1
.

4.1
Linked pairs of projections

Definition 4.1
.

Let
P
be a projection algebra, and let
p\in P
. A pair of projections
(e,f)\in P\times P
is said to be
p
-linked
if

f=e\theta_{p}\theta_{f}\and e=f\theta_{p}\theta_{e}.

(4.2)

Associated to such a
p
-linked pair
(e,f)
we define the tuples

\lambda(e,p,f)=(e,e\theta_{p},f)\and\rho(e,p,f)=(e,f\theta_{p},f).

The next two results gather some important basic properties of
p
-linked pairs. Recall that
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(P)
denotes the path category of
P
.

Lemma 4.3
.

If
(e,f)
is
p
-linked, then

(i)

(f,e)
is also
p
-linked, and we have
\lambda(e,p,f)^{\textup{rev}}=\rho(f,p,e)
and
\rho(e,p,f)^{\textup{rev}}=\lambda(f,p,e)
,

(ii)

e,f\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}p
,

(iii)

Each of
e
and
f
is
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related to each of
e\theta_{p}
and
f\theta_{p}
; consequently both
\lambda(e,p,f)
and
\rho(e,p,f)
belong to
\mathscr{P}(e,f)
.

Proof.

(i)
. This follows directly by inspecting Definition
4.1
.

(ii)
. By the symmetry afforded by part
(i)
, it suffices to show that
e\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}p
. For this we use (
4.2
), Lemma
3.4
and
(P3)
to calculate

p\theta_{e}=p\theta_{f\theta_{p}\theta_{e}}=p\theta_{e}\theta_{p}\theta_{f}\theta_{p}\theta_{e}=e\theta_{p}\theta_{f}\theta_{p}\theta_{e}=f\theta_{p}\theta_{e}=e.

(iii)
. By symmetry, it suffices to show that
e\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}e\theta_{p}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}f
. Combining
{e\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}p}
with
(PA1)
, it follows that
e=p\theta_{e}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}e\theta_{p}
. We obtain
f\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}e\theta_{p}
directly from (
4.2
). Using
(P4)
and (
4.2
) we calculate
f\theta_{e\theta_{p}}=f\theta_{p}\theta_{e}\theta_{p}=e\theta_{p}
, so that
e\theta_{p}\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}f
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Lemma
4.3
(iii)
says that
(e,f)
being
p
-linked implies
e,f\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}e\theta_{p},f\theta_{p}
. The converse of this holds as well, as (
4.2
) says that
f\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}e\theta_{p}
and
e\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}f\theta_{p}
. Thus, we could take
{e,f\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}e\theta_{p},f\theta_{p}}
as an equivalent definition for
(e,f)
to be
p
-linked.

Remark 4.5
.

Consider a projection
p\in P
, and a
p
-linked pair
(e,f)
. By Lemma
4.3
(ii)
we have
e,f\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}p
, and of course we also have
e\theta_{p},f\theta_{p}\leq p
. These relationships are all shown in Figure
2
. In the diagram, each arrow
s\to t
stands for the
P
-path
(s,t)\in\mathscr{P}
. Thus, the upper and lower paths from
e
to
f

correspond to
\lambda(e,p,f)
and
\rho(e,p,f)
, respectively.

e
p
e\theta_{p}
f\theta_{p}
f

Figure 2:
A projection
p\in P
, and a
p
-linked pair
(e,f)
, as in Definition
4.1
. Dotted and dashed lines indicate
\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
and
\leq
relationships, respectively.
Lines with arrows indicate the paths
\lambda(e,p,f)
and
\rho(e,p,f)
. Of course, by Lemma
4.3
(i)
, the dual diagram in which all four arrows are reversed is also valid.
See Remark
4.5
for more details.

Lemma 4.6
.

If
(e,f)
is
p
-linked, and if
e^{\prime}\leq e
, then
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
is
p
-linked, where
f^{\prime}=e^{\prime}\theta_{p}\theta_{f}
, and we have

{}_{e^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}\lambda(e,p,f)=\lambda(e^{\prime},p,f^{\prime})\and{}_{e^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}\rho(e,p,f)=\rho(e^{\prime},p,f^{\prime}).

Proof.

To show that
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
is
p
-linked, we must show that
f^{\prime}=e^{\prime}\theta_{p}\theta_{f^{\prime}}
and
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}\theta_{p}\theta_{e^{\prime}}
. For the first we use the definition of
f^{\prime}
and Lemma
3.4
several times to calculate

e^{\prime}\theta_{p}\theta_{f^{\prime}}=e^{\prime}\theta_{p}\theta_{e^{\prime}\theta_{p}\theta_{f}}=e^{\prime}\theta_{p}\theta_{f}\theta_{p}\theta_{e^{\prime}}\theta_{p}\theta_{f}=f\theta_{p}\theta_{e^{\prime}}\theta_{p}\theta_{f}={e^{\prime}}\theta_{p}\theta_{f}=f^{\prime}.

For the second we have

\displaystyle f^{\prime}\theta_{p}\theta_{e^{\prime}}=e^{\prime}\theta_{p}\theta_{f}\theta_{p}\theta_{e^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=f\theta_{p}\theta_{e^{\prime}}

by definition, and by Lemma
3.4

\displaystyle=f\theta_{p}\theta_{e}\theta_{e^{\prime}}

by
(PA4)
, as
e^{\prime}\leq e

\displaystyle=e\theta_{e^{\prime}}

by (
4.2
)

\displaystyle=e^{\prime}

as
e^{\prime}\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}e
by
(PA3)
.

We now show that
{}_{e^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}\lambda(e,p,f)=\lambda(e^{\prime},p,f^{\prime})
, the proof that
{}_{e^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}\rho(e,p,f)=\rho(e^{\prime},p,f^{\prime})
being analogous. Using (
3.5
) and Definition
4.1
, we have

{}_{e^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}\lambda(e,p,f)=(e^{\prime},e^{\prime}\theta_{e\theta_{p}},e^{\prime}\theta_{e\theta_{p}}\theta_{f})\and\lambda(e^{\prime},p,f^{\prime})=(e^{\prime},e^{\prime}\theta_{p},f^{\prime}).

Both have first component
e^{\prime}
. We deduce equality of the second and third components from the following calculations:

•

e^{\prime}\theta_{e\theta_{p}}=e^{\prime}\theta_{p}\theta_{e}\theta_{p}=e^{\prime}\theta_{e}\theta_{p}\theta_{e}\theta_{p}=e^{\prime}\theta_{e}\theta_{p}=e^{\prime}\theta_{p}
, using
e^{\prime}\leq e
,
(P4)
and
(P5)
,

•

(e^{\prime}\theta_{e\theta_{p}})\theta_{f}=e^{\prime}\theta_{p}\theta_{f}=f^{\prime}
, using the previous calculation, and the definition of
f^{\prime}
. ∎

4.2
The reduced chain groupoid

We now use linked pairs to define the reduced chain groupoid of a projection algebra.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
P
be a projection algebra, and let
\Xi=\Xi(P)
be the set of all pairs
{(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\mathscr{P}\times\mathscr{P}}
of the forms
(

Ω

1)
and
(

Ω

2)
, as well as:

(
\mathsf{\Omega}
3)

\mathfrak{s}=\lambda(e,p,f)
and
\mathfrak{t}=\rho(e,p,f)
, for some
p\in P
, and some
p
-linked pair
(e,f)
.

Let
{\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}}=\Xi^{\sharp}
be the congruence on
\mathscr{P}
generated by
\Xi
, and define the quotient

\overline{\mathscr{C}}=\overline{\mathscr{C}}(P)=\mathscr{P}/{\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}}.

As
\Omega\subseteq\Xi
, we have
{\approx}\subseteq{\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}}
, and so
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is a quotient of the chain groupoid
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)=\mathscr{P}/{\approx}
. As such
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is itself a groupoid, which we call the
reduced chain groupoid of
P
. The elements of
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
are called
reduced (
P
-)chains
, and we denote by
[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]
the reduced chain containing the path
{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}}
. We denote the quotient map
\mathscr{C}\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}
by

\nu:\mathscr{C}\to\overline{\mathscr{C}},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{which is given by}\hskip 20.00003pt\nu[\mathfrak{p}]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}$.}

Remark 4.8
.

We will see in Subsection
10.1
that there exists a natural (and generally smaller) subset of
\Xi
that also generates the congruence
\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}
. For now it is more convenient to use
\Xi
, as its definition is more symmetrical.

In Proposition
4.13
we show that
(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)
is a chained projection groupoid. We build towards this with a number of lemmas.

Lemma 4.9
.

\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}
is an ordered
*
-congruence, and
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is an ordered groupoid.

Proof.

We have already observed that
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is a groupoid.
As explained in Section
3.1
, we can show that
{\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}}=\Xi^{\sharp}
is an ordered
*
-congruence by showing that

\mathfrak{s}^{\textup{rev}}\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}\mathfrak{t}^{\textup{rev}}\and{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{s}\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}{}_{p}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{t}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\Xi$, and all $p\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{s})$.}

When
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\Xi
has type
(

Ω

1)
or
(

Ω

2)
, this was done in
[
26
, Lemma 5.16]
. For pairs of type
(

Ω

3)
we apply Lemmas
4.3
and
4.6
.
∎

The order in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is determined by the restrictions, which are inherited from
\mathscr{C}
(and hence ultimately from
\mathscr{P}
). That is, for
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
they are given by

{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\and[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]{\downharpoonright}_{r}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}{\downharpoonright}_{r}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $q\leq{\bf d}(\mathfrak{p})={\bf d}[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]$ and $r\leq{\bf r}(\mathfrak{p})={\bf r}[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]$.}

These are well defined by Lemma
4.9

It is clear that
(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}})
is a weak projection groupoid. To show that it is a projection groupoid we need to verify
(G1)
. To do so, we need to understand the maps
\Theta_{\mathfrak{c}}=\theta_{{\bf d}(\mathfrak{c})}\vartheta_{\mathfrak{c}}
.

Lemma 4.10
.

For
\mathfrak{c}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
, we have
\Theta_{\mathfrak{c}}=\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}
.

Proof.

Using (
3.5
), and writing
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\in\mathscr{P}
, we first calculate

q\vartheta_{\mathfrak{c}}={\bf r}({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{c})={\bf r}({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt])={\bf r}[\hskip-1.5649pt[{}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]={\bf r}({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{p})=q\theta_{p_{2}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $q\leq p_{1}={\bf d}(\mathfrak{c})$.}

It follows from this that
t\Theta_{\mathfrak{c}}=t\theta_{{\bf d}(\mathfrak{c})}\vartheta_{\mathfrak{c}}=t\theta_{p_{1}}\theta_{p_{2}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}
for arbitrary
t\in P
.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

If
P
is a projection algebra, then
(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}})
is a projection groupoid.

Proof.

To verify
(G1)
, consider a reduced chain
\mathfrak{c}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
, and let
q\in P
.
Then by Lemmas
4.10
and
3.4
we have

\theta_{q\Theta_{\mathfrak{c}}}=\theta_{q\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}}=\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}}\theta_{q}\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}=\Theta_{\mathfrak{c}^{-1}}\theta_{q}\Theta_{\mathfrak{c}}.\qed

We now bring in the quotient map
\nu:\mathscr{C}\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}
from Definition
4.7
.

Lemma 4.12
.

If
P
is a projection algebra, then
\nu
is an evaluation map.

Proof.

Clearly
\nu
is a
v
-functor. To see that it is ordered, consider a path
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\in\mathscr{P}
, and let
q\leq p_{1}
.
Then with the
q_{i}
as in (
3.5
), we have

\nu({}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}[\mathfrak{p}])=\nu[q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}]\hskip-1.5649pt]={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\hskip-1.5649pt]={}_{q}{\downharpoonleft}(\nu[\mathfrak{p}]).\qed

Proposition 4.13
.

If
P
is a projection algebra, then
(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)
is a chained projection groupoid.

Proof.

It remains to verify
(G2)
. To do so, fix a
\mathfrak{c}
-linked pair
(e,f)
, where
\mathfrak{c}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\in\overline{\mathscr{C}}
.
So

f=e\Theta_{\mathfrak{c}}\theta_{f}\and e=f\Theta_{\mathfrak{c}^{-1}}\theta_{e},

(4.14)

and we must show that
\lambda(e,\mathfrak{c},f)=\rho(e,\mathfrak{c},f)
. Keeping
\varepsilon=\nu
in mind, these morphisms are defined by

\lambda(e,\mathfrak{c},f)=[\hskip-1.5649pt[e,e_{1}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\circ{}_{e_{1}}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{c}\circ[\hskip-1.5649pt[f_{1},f]\hskip-1.5649pt]\and\rho(e,\mathfrak{c},f)=[\hskip-1.5649pt[e,e_{2}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\circ\mathfrak{c}{\downharpoonright}_{f_{2}}\circ[\hskip-1.5649pt[f_{2},f]\hskip-1.5649pt],

(4.15)

in terms of the projections

e_{1}=e\theta_{p_{1}},\hskip 20.00003pte_{2}=f\Theta_{\mathfrak{c}^{-1}},\hskip 20.00003ptf_{1}=e\Theta_{\mathfrak{c}}\and f_{2}=f\theta_{p_{k}}.

For convenience, we will write

{}_{e_{1}}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{c}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\and\mathfrak{c}{\downharpoonright}_{f_{2}}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}]\hskip-1.5649pt].

Using (
3.5
), and
e_{1}=e\theta_{p_{1}}
and
f_{2}=f\theta_{p_{k}}
, we have

u_{i}=e_{1}\theta_{p_{2}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}=e\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}\and v_{i}=f_{2}\theta_{p_{k-1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}=f\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}},

for each
i
. Keeping in mind that the compositions in (
4.15
) exist, we have

\lambda(e,\mathfrak{c},f)=[\hskip-1.5649pt[e,u_{1},\ldots,u_{k},f]\hskip-1.5649pt]\and\rho(e,\mathfrak{c},f)=[\hskip-1.5649pt[e,v_{1},\ldots,v_{k},f]\hskip-1.5649pt],

and we must show that these are equal. It will also be convenient to additionally write
u_{0}=e
and
v_{k+1}=f
. To assist with understanding the coming arguments, these projections are shown in Figure
3
(in the case
k=4
). Our task is essentially to show that the large ‘rectangle’ at the bottom of the diagram commutes, modulo
\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}
.

We recall the notion of
p
-linked pairs (Definition
4.1
) and claim that:

(u_{i-1},v_{i+1})\text{ is }p_{i}\text{-linked for each }1\leq i\leq k.

(4.16)

To prove this, we must show that

v_{i+1}=u_{i-1}\theta_{p_{i}}\theta_{v_{i+1}}\and u_{i-1}=v_{i+1}\theta_{p_{i}}\theta_{u_{i-1}}.

First we note that (
4.14
) and Lemma
4.10
give

f=e\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}\theta_{f}\and e=f\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{1}}\theta_{e}.

Combining this with Lemma
3.4
, we obtain

\displaystyle u_{i-1}\theta_{p_{i}}\theta_{v_{i+1}}

\displaystyle=e\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i-1}}\cdot\theta_{p_{i}}\cdot\theta_{f\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i+1}}}

\displaystyle=e\theta_{p_{1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i-1}}\cdot\theta_{p_{i}}\cdot\theta_{p_{i+1}}\cdots\theta_{p_{k}}\theta_{f}\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i+1}}=f\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i+1}}=v_{i+1}.

The proof that
u_{i-1}=v_{i+1}\theta_{p_{i}}\theta_{u_{i-1}}
is analogous, and (
4.16
) is proved.
Now, the linked pairs in (
4.16
) lead to the paths

\displaystyle\lambda(u_{i-1},p_{i},v_{i+1})

\displaystyle=(u_{i-1},u_{i-1}\theta_{p_{i}},v_{i+1})

and

\displaystyle\rho(u_{i-1},p_{i},v_{i+1})

\displaystyle=(u_{i-1},v_{i+1}\theta_{p_{i}},v_{i+1})

\displaystyle=(u_{i-1},u_{i},v_{i+1})

\displaystyle=(u_{i-1},v_{i},v_{i+1}),

as in Definition
4.1
. Since
(\lambda(u_{i-1},p_{i},v_{i+1}),\rho(u_{i-1},p_{i},v_{i+1}))
is a pair of the form
(

Ω

3)
, we have

[\hskip-1.5649pt[u_{i-1},v_{i},v_{i+1}]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[u_{i-1},u_{i},v_{i+1}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for each $1\leq i\leq k$.}

(4.17)

In other words, each of the small rectangles at the bottom of Figure
3
commutes, and then it follows that the large rectangle commutes. Formally, we repeatedly use (
4.17
) in the indicated places, as follows:

\displaystyle\rho(e,\mathfrak{c},f)=[\hskip-1.5649pt[e,v_{1},\ldots,v_{k},f]\hskip-1.5649pt]

\displaystyle=[\hskip-1.5649pt[\underline{u_{0},v_{1},v_{2}},v_{3},v_{4},\ldots,v_{k},v_{k+1}]\hskip-1.5649pt]

\displaystyle=[\hskip-1.5649pt[u_{0},\underline{u_{1},v_{2},v_{3}},v_{4},\ldots,v_{k},v_{k+1}]\hskip-1.5649pt]

\displaystyle=[\hskip-1.5649pt[u_{0},u_{1},\underline{u_{2},v_{3},v_{4}},\ldots,v_{k},v_{k+1}]\hskip-1.5649pt]

\displaystyle\hskip 3.69885pt\vdots

\displaystyle=[\hskip-1.5649pt[u_{0},u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4},\ldots,u_{k},v_{k+1}]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[e,u_{1},\ldots,u_{k},f]\hskip-1.5649pt]=\lambda(e,\mathfrak{c},f),

and the proof is complete.
∎

u_{0}
v_{5}
=f_{\phantom{0}}
p_{1}
u_{1}

p_{2}
