Figure 3:
The projections
e,f,p_{i},u_{i},v_{i}
from the proof of Proposition
4.13
, shown here in the case
k=4
. Dashed lines indicate
\leq
relationships. Each arrow
s\to t
represents the
P
-path
(s,t)\in\mathscr{P}
, so the upper and lower paths
e\to f
represent
\lambda(e,\mathfrak{c},f)=(e,u_{1},\ldots,u_{k},f)
and
\rho(e,\mathfrak{c},f)=(e,v_{1},\ldots,v_{k},f)
, respectively.

For a projection algebra
P
, we write
{\bf F}(P)=(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)
for the chained projection groupoid from Proposition
4.13
. The assignment
P\mapsto{\bf F}(P)
can be thought of as an object map
{{\bf PA}\to{\bf CPG}}
. We can extend this to morphisms as well. Indeed, fix a projection algebra morphism
\phi:P\to P^{\prime}
. In what follows we use the standard abbreviations for the constructions associated to
P
, and use dashes to distinguish those for
P^{\prime}
; e.g.
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(P)
and
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}=\mathscr{C}(P^{\prime})
. We first define a mapping

\varphi:\mathscr{P}\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{by}\hskip 20.00003pt\mathfrak{p}\varphi=\nu^{\prime}([\mathfrak{p}]\mathscr{C}(\phi)),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{i.e.}\hskip 20.00003pt(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\varphi=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1}\phi,\ldots,p_{k}\phi]\hskip-1.5649pt].

(4.18)

Note that
\varphi
is the composite of three ordered
*
-functors, namely the quotient map
\mathscr{P}\to\mathscr{C}
, followed by
{\mathscr{C}(\phi):\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}
, and then the evaluation
\nu^{\prime}:\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}
.
Hence
\varphi
is itself an ordered
*
-functor. It is straightforward to verify that
\Xi\subseteq\ker(\varphi)
, meaning that
\mathfrak{s}\varphi=\mathfrak{t}\varphi
for all
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\Xi
. Therefore there is a well-defined ordered groupoid functor

\displaystyle{\bf F}(\phi):\overline{\mathscr{C}}\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]{\bf F}(\phi)=\mathfrak{p}\varphi=\nu^{\prime}([\mathfrak{p}]\mathscr{C}(\phi)),

i.e.

\displaystyle[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\hskip-1.5649pt]{\bf F}(\phi)=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1}\phi,\ldots,p_{k}\phi]\hskip-1.5649pt].

Proposition 4.19
.

{\bf F}
is a functor
{\bf PA}\to{\bf CPG}
.

Proof.

We first verify that
{\bf F}(\phi)
, as above, is a chained projection functor from
{{\bf F}(P)=(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)}
to
{\bf F}(P^{\prime})=(P^{\prime},\overline{\mathscr{C}}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
. The restriction of
{\bf F}(\phi)
to
P=v\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is
\phi
, which is a projection algebra morphism by assumption. To show that
{\bf F}(\phi)
preserves evaluation maps we need to check that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(P)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(P^{\prime})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathscr{C}}(P)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathscr{C}}(P^{\prime}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf F}(\phi)}

and this is routine. It is also routine to check that
{\bf F}(\phi\circ\phi^{\prime})={\bf F}(\phi)\circ{\bf F}(\phi^{\prime})
for composable morphisms
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
, and that
{\bf F}(\operatorname{id}_{P})=\operatorname{id}_{{\bf F}(P)}
for all
P
.
∎

4.3
The chain semigroup

As a result of Proposition
4.13
and Theorem
3.9
, we have a regular
*
-semigroup
{\bf S}(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)
, which we will denote by
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
, and call the
chain semigroup of
P
. The choice of notation, which harkens back to the free idempotent-generated semigroups
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
, will be justified by the results of Section
5
. In line with Subsection
3.5
, we denote the product in
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)={\bf S}(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)
by
\bullet
. To give an explicit description of
\bullet
, consider an arbitrary pair of reduced chains
\mathfrak{c}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\hskip-1.5649pt]
and
\mathfrak{d}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[q_{1},\ldots,q_{l}]\hskip-1.5649pt]
, and write
p={\bf r}(\mathfrak{c})=p_{k}
and
q={\bf d}(\mathfrak{d})=q_{1}
. As in (
3.8
), and remembering that the evaluation map is
\nu
, we have

\mathfrak{c}\bullet\mathfrak{d}=\mathfrak{c}{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}\circ[\hskip-1.5649pt[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\circ{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{d}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptp^{\prime}=q\theta_{p}\and q^{\prime}=p\theta_{q}.

Using (
3.5
), we have
\mathfrak{c}{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{k}^{\prime}]\hskip-1.5649pt]
and
{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{d}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[q_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,q_{l}^{\prime}]\hskip-1.5649pt]
, where

p_{i}^{\prime}=p^{\prime}\theta_{p_{k}}\cdots\theta_{p_{i}}\and q_{j}^{\prime}=q^{\prime}\theta_{q_{1}}\cdots\theta_{q_{j}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $1\leq i\leq k$ and $1\leq j\leq l$,}

and where
p^{\prime}=p_{k}^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}=q_{1}^{\prime}
. Again, these restrictions are well defined because of Lemma
4.9
. It follows that

\mathfrak{c}\bullet\mathfrak{d}=\mathfrak{c}{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}\circ[\hskip-1.5649pt[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\circ{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{d}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{k}^{\prime}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\circ[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{k}^{\prime},q_{1}^{\prime}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\circ[\hskip-1.5649pt[q_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,q_{l}^{\prime}]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{k}^{\prime},q_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,q_{l}^{\prime}]\hskip-1.5649pt]

is simply the
concatenation
of
\mathfrak{c}{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}
and
{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{d}
, which we denote by
\mathfrak{c}{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}\oplus{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{d}
.
As special cases we have

\mathfrak{c}\bullet\mathfrak{d}=\mathfrak{c}\oplus\mathfrak{d}
if
{\bf r}(\mathfrak{c})\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}{\bf d}(\mathfrak{d})
and
\mathfrak{c}\bullet\mathfrak{d}=\mathfrak{c}\circ\mathfrak{d}
if
{\bf r}(\mathfrak{c})={\bf d}(\mathfrak{d})
.

(4.20)

Definition 4.21
.

The
chain semigroup

\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
of a projection algebra
P
, is the regular
*
-semigroup defined as follows.

(CP1)

The elements of
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
are the reduced (
P
-)chains,
[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\hskip-1.5649pt]
, as in Definition
4.7
.

(CP2)

The product
\bullet
in
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is defined, for
\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{d}\in\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
with
p={\bf r}(\mathfrak{c})
and
q={\bf d}(\mathfrak{d})
, by

\mathfrak{c}\bullet\mathfrak{d}=\mathfrak{c}{\downharpoonright}_{p^{\prime}}\oplus{}_{q^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}\mathfrak{d},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptp^{\prime}=q\theta_{p}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptq^{\prime}=p\theta_{q},

and where
\oplus
denotes concatenation, as above.

(CP3)

The involution in
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is given by
[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\hskip-1.5649pt]^{*}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{k},\ldots,p_{1}]\hskip-1.5649pt]
.

(CP4)

The projections of
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
have the form
[\hskip-1.5649pt[p]\hskip-1.5649pt]=p
, for
p\in P
, and consequently
{{\bf P}(\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P))=P}
. Moreover, these projection algebras have the same operations, as

p\bullet q\bullet p=q\theta_{p}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $p,q\in P$.}

(CP5)

The idempotents of
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
have the form
p\bullet q=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]
, for
(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
.

We have proved the following.

Theorem 4.22
.

If
P
is a projection algebra, then its chain semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is a projection-generated (equivalently, idempotent-generated) regular
*
-semigroup whose projection algebra is precisely
P
. ∎

We have now introduced the main concept of our study, the chain semigroup associated to a projection algebra, and the remaining sections of the paper investigate these semigroups from a number of different angles:

•

their incarnation as free objects in the category of regular
*
-semigroups (Section
5
),

•

their idempotent/biordered structure (Section
6
),

•

their presentations by generators and defining relations (Section
7
),

•

their relationship to free (regular) idempotent-generated semigroups (Section
7
), and

•

their topological structure (Section
10
).

In Sections
8
and
9
we present a number of natural examples.

5
Freeness of the chain semigroup

In the previous section we showed how to construct the chain semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)={\bf S}(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)
from a projection algebra
P
. Now we will explain how
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is rightfully thought of as ‘the free regular
*
-semigroup with projection algebra
P
’. In categorical language, this is to say that the chain semigroups are the objects in the image of a left adjoint to the forgetful functor
{\bf P}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}
from (
3.2
). (The precise meanings of these terms are given below.) The forgetful functor in question maps a regular
*
-semigroup
S
to its projection algebra
{\bf P}(S)
. It follows from Proposition
4.19
(and the isomorphism
{\bf RSS}\cong{\bf CPG}
) that the assignment
P\mapsto\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is the object part of a functor
{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSS}
. Our main goal here is to prove the following result (where again definitions are given below).

Theorem 5.1
.

The functor
{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSS}:P\mapsto\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is a left adjoint to the forgetful functor
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}:S\mapsto{\bf P}(S)
, and
{\bf PA}
is coreflective in
{\bf RSS}
.

In fact, it will be more convenient to prove the following groupoid version of Theorem
5.1
; the two theorems are equivalent via the isomorphism
{\bf RSS}\cong{\bf CPG}
.

Theorem 5.2
.

The functor
{\bf PA}\to{\bf CPG}:P\mapsto(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)
is a left adjoint to the forgetful functor
{\bf CPG}\to{\bf PA}:(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)\mapsto P
, and
{\bf PA}
is coreflective in
{\bf CPG}
.

We now give the (standard) definitions of the terms appearing in the above results; for more details see
[
5
,
46
]
.

Definition 5.3
.

Consider two categories
{\bf C}
and
{\bf D}
, and a pair of functors
{{\bf F},{\bf G}:{\bf C}\to{\bf D}}
. A
natural transformation

\eta:{\bf F}\to{\bf G}
is a family
\eta=(\eta_{C})_{C\in v{\bf C}}
, where each
\eta_{C}:{\bf F}(C)\to{\bf G}(C)
is a morphism in
{\bf D}
, and such that the following condition holds:

•

For every pair of objects
C,C^{\prime}\in v{\bf C}
, and for every morphism
\phi:C\to C^{\prime}
in
{\bf C}
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf F}(C)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf F}(C^{\prime})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf G}(C)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf G}(C^{\prime}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf F}(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{C^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf G}(\phi)}

We call
\eta
a
natural isomorphism
if each
\eta_{C}
is an isomorphism (in
{\bf D}
), in which case we write
{{\bf F}\cong{\bf G}}
.

Definition 5.4
.

Consider two categories
{\bf C}
and
{\bf D}
. An
adjunction

{\bf C}\to{\bf D}
is a triple
({\bf F},{\bf U},\eta)
, where
{\bf F}:{\bf C}\to{\bf D}
and
{\bf U}:{\bf D}\to{\bf C}
are functors, and
\eta
is a natural transformation
\operatorname{id}_{\bf C}\to{\bf U}{\bf F}
, such that the following condition holds:

•

For every pair of objects
C\in v{\bf C}
and
D\in v{\bf D}
, and for every morphism
\phi:C\to{\bf U}(D)
in
{\bf C}
, there exists a unique morphism
\overline{\phi}:{\bf F}(C)\to D
in
{\bf D}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf U}({\bf F}(C))}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf U}(D).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf U}(\overline{\phi})}

In this set-up,
{\bf F}
and
{\bf U}
are called the
left
and
right adjoints
, respectively, and
\eta
is the
unit
of the adjunction.
The
{\bf U}
-free objects in
{\bf D}
are the objects in the image of
{\bf F}
, i.e. those of the form
{\bf F}(C)
for
C\in v{\bf C}
.

We are particularly interested in special adjunctions where we actually have the
equality

{{\bf U}{\bf F}=\operatorname{id}_{{\bf C}}}
. Note that
\operatorname{id}=(\operatorname{id}_{C})_{C\in v{\bf C}}
is clearly a natural transformation
\operatorname{id}_{\bf C}\to\operatorname{id}_{\bf C}
for any category
{\bf C}
. Lemma
5.6
below concerns this situation, and speaks of so-called
coreflective
(sub)categories.

Definition 5.5
.

A
coreflective subcategory
of a category
{\bf D}
is a full subcategory
{\bf B}
whose inclusion functor
{\bf B}\to{\bf D}
has a right adjoint. We say a category is
coreflective in
{\bf D}
if it is isomorphic to a coreflective subcategory of
{\bf D}
. That is,
{\bf C}
is coreflective in
{\bf D}
if there is a full embedding
{\bf C}\to{\bf D}
that has a right adjoint.

In the above, a subcategory
{\bf B}
of
{\bf D}
is
full
if it contains every morphism of
{\bf D}
between objects of
{\bf B}
. A
full embedding
is a functor
{\bf C}\to{\bf D}
that is injective on objects and morphisms, and whose image is full in
{\bf D}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose
{\bf C}
and
{\bf D}
are categories, and
{\bf F}:{\bf C}\to{\bf D}
and
{\bf U}:{\bf D}\to{\bf C}
are functors with
{\bf U}{\bf F}=\operatorname{id}_{{\bf C}}
, for which the following condition holds:

•

For every pair of objects
C\in v{\bf C}
and
D\in v{\bf D}
, and for every morphism
\phi:C\to{\bf U}(D)
in
{\bf C}
, there exists a unique morphism
\overline{\phi}:{\bf F}(C)\to D
in
{\bf D}
such that
\phi={\bf U}(\overline{\phi})
.

Then

(i)

({\bf F},{\bf U},\operatorname{id})
is an adjunction,

(ii)

{\bf C}\cong{\bf F}({\bf C})
is coreflective in
{\bf D}
.

Proof.

(i)
. This is a direct translation of Definition
5.4
in the special case that
{\bf U}{\bf F}=\operatorname{id}_{\bf C}
and
\eta=\operatorname{id}
.

(ii)
. This follows from the fact that all components of the unit
\operatorname{id}=(\operatorname{id}_{C})
are isomorphisms; see for example
[
46
, Theorem IV.3.1]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.2
.

We denote the functors in question by

{\bf F}:{\bf PA}\to{\bf CPG}:P\mapsto(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)\and{\bf U}:{\bf CPG}\to{\bf PA}:(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)\mapsto P.

We prove the theorem by applying Lemma
5.6
. It is clear that
{\bf U}{\bf F}=\operatorname{id}_{\bf PA}
, so we are left to verify the following condition:

•

For every projection algebra
P
, every chained projection groupoid
(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
, and every projection algebra morphism
{\phi:P\to{\bf U}(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)=P^{\prime}}
, there exists a unique chained projection functor
\overline{\phi}:{\bf F}(P)=(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)\to(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
such that
\phi={\bf U}(\overline{\phi})
.

To establish the existence of
\overline{\phi}
, we first define

\varphi:\mathscr{P}\to\mathcal{G}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{by}\hskip 20.00003pt\mathfrak{p}\varphi=\varepsilon([\mathfrak{p}]\mathscr{C}(\phi)),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{i.e. }(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\varphi=\varepsilon[p_{1}\phi,\ldots,p_{k}\phi]\text{ for }\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}.

where here
{\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(P)}
is the path category of
P
.
As with (
4.18
),
\varphi
is the composite of three ordered
*
-morphisms, and is hence itself an ordered
*
-morphism. We next show that
\Xi\subseteq\ker(\varphi)
, i.e. that

\mathfrak{s}\varphi=\mathfrak{t}\varphi\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\Xi$.}

(5.7)

This is clear when
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})
has the form
(

Ω

1)
or
(

Ω

2)
. Now suppose
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})
has the form
(

Ω

3)
, so that

\mathfrak{s}=\lambda(e,p,f)=(e,e\theta_{p},f)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{t}=\rho(e,p,f)=(e,f\theta_{p},f)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $p$-linked pair $(e,f)$.}

We show that:

(i)

(e\phi,f\phi)
is
b
-linked in
\mathcal{G}
for the morphism
b=p\phi\in\mathcal{G}
, and

(ii)

\lambda(e\phi,b,f\phi)=\mathfrak{s}\varphi
and
\rho(e\phi,b,f\phi)=\mathfrak{t}\varphi
.

Since
\lambda(e\phi,b,f\phi)=\rho(e\phi,b,f\phi)
in
\mathcal{G}
, this will complete the proof that
\mathfrak{s}\varphi=\mathfrak{t}\varphi
.
For
(i)
we need to show that

f\phi=(e\phi)\Theta_{b}\theta_{f\phi}\and e\phi=(f\phi)\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{e\phi}.

Since
p\phi\in P^{\prime}
is a projection, we have
\Theta_{b}=\Theta_{p\phi}=\theta_{p\phi}
by
[
26
, equation (6.3)]
. Combined with the fact that
\phi
is a projection algebra morphism, it follows that

(e\phi)\Theta_{b}\theta_{f\phi}=(e\phi)\theta_{p\phi}\theta_{f\phi}=(e\theta_{p}\theta_{f})\phi=f\phi.

An analogous calculation (keeping in mind
b^{-1}=(p\phi)^{-1}=p\phi
) gives
e\phi=(f\phi)\Theta_{b^{-1}}\theta_{e\phi}
, and completes the proof of
(i)
.
For
(ii)
we first note that

\displaystyle\lambda(e\phi,p\phi,f\phi)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[e\phi,e_{1}]\circ{}_{e_{1}}{\downharpoonleft}(p\phi)\circ\varepsilon[f_{1},f\phi]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[e\phi,e_{1}]\circ e_{1}\circ\varepsilon[f_{1},f\phi]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon[e\phi,e_{1}]\circ\varepsilon[f_{1},f\phi],

where
e_{1}=(e\phi)\theta_{p\phi}=(e\theta_{p})\phi
and
f_{1}=(e\phi)\Theta_{p\phi}=(e\phi)\theta_{p\phi}=(e\theta_{p})\phi\ (=e_{1})
. Thus, continuing from above we have

\lambda(e\phi,p\phi,f\phi)=\varepsilon[e\phi,e_{1}]\circ\varepsilon[f_{1},f\phi]=\varepsilon[e\phi,(e\theta_{p})\phi]\circ\varepsilon[(e\theta_{p})\phi,f\phi]=\varepsilon[e\phi,(e\theta_{p})\phi,f\phi]=\mathfrak{s}\varphi.

Analogously,
\rho(e\phi,p\phi,f\phi)=\mathfrak{t}\varphi
, completing the proof of
(ii)
.

Now that we have proved (
5.7
), it follows that there is a well-defined ordered groupoid functor

\overline{\phi}:\overline{\mathscr{C}}(=\mathscr{P}/{\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}})\to\mathcal{G}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{given by}\hskip 20.00003pt[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\overline{\phi}=\mathfrak{p}\varphi{}=\varepsilon([\mathfrak{p}]\mathscr{C}(\phi))\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}$.}

We next check that
\overline{\phi}
is in fact a chained projection functor
(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)\to(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)
, for which we need to show that:

(iii)

the object map
v\overline{\phi}=\overline{\phi}|_{P}
is a projection algebra morphism
P\to P^{\prime}
, and

(iv)

\overline{\phi}
respects the evaluation maps, in the sense that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(P)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(P^{\prime})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathscr{C}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(v\overline{\phi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi}}

For
(iii)
, we note that
p\overline{\phi}=p\varphi=\varepsilon[p\phi]=p\phi
for all
p\in P
, so that
v\overline{\phi}=\phi
is a projection algebra morphism by assumption. For
(iv)
, if
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
then

\varepsilon([\mathfrak{p}]\mathscr{C}(\phi))=\mathfrak{p}\varphi=[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\overline{\phi}=(\nu[\mathfrak{p}])\overline{\phi}.

So
\overline{\phi}
is indeed a chained projection functor, and it follows from the proof of
(iii)
above that
{\bf U}(\overline{\phi})=\overline{\phi}|_{P}=\phi
.

We have now established the existence of
\overline{\phi}
. For uniqueness, suppose the chained projection functor
{\psi:(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)\to(P^{\prime},\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)}
also satisfies
{\bf U}(\psi)=\phi
.
Then for any
{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}}
we have

\displaystyle[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\psi=(\nu[\mathfrak{p}])\psi

\displaystyle=\varepsilon([\mathfrak{p}]\mathscr{C}(v\psi))

since
\psi
respects evaluation maps

\displaystyle=\varepsilon([\mathfrak{p}]\mathscr{C}(\phi))

since
v\psi={\bf U}(\psi)=\phi

\displaystyle=[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\overline{\phi},

and so
\psi=\overline{\phi}
.
∎

Now that we have proved Theorem
5.1
(via Theorem
5.2
and the isomorphism
{\bf RSS}\cong{\bf CPG}
), it follows that the chain semigroups are the
{\bf P}
-free objects in
{\bf RSS}
.
Henceforth, we call
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
the
free (projection-generated) regular
*
-semigroup over the projection algebra
P
.
As noted earlier, the notation is inspired by an analogy with the free (idempotent-generated) semigroup over a biordered set
E
, which is denoted
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
. The relationship between
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
and
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
will be a key topic for the remainder of the paper, starting in Section
6
.

We conclude the current section by drawing out two purely semigroup-theoretical results.

Theorem 5.8
.

If
P
is a projection algebra, then

(i)

\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is a regular
*
-semigroup with projection algebra
P
,

(ii)

for any regular
*
-semigroup
S
, and any projection algebra morphism
\phi:P\to{\bf P}(S)
, there is a unique
*
-semigroup homomorphism
{\overline{\phi}:\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\to S}
such that the following diagram commutes (where both vertical maps are inclusions):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf P}(S)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \ \phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi}\ \ }

and moreover any
*
-semigroup homomorphism
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\to S
has the form
\overline{\phi}
for some projection algebra morphism
\phi:P\to{\bf P}(S)
.

Proof.

(i)
. This is contained in Theorem
4.22
.

(ii)
. The existence and uniqueness of
\overline{\phi}
follows from the proof of Theorem
5.2
and the isomorphism
{\bf RSS}\cong{\bf CPG}
. Given any
*
-semigroup morphism
\psi:\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\to S
, we have
\psi=\overline{v\psi}
.
∎

The previous theorem has the following consequence:

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
P
and
P^{\prime}
be projection algebras. Then for any projection algebra morphism
\phi:P\to P^{\prime}
, there is a unique
*
-semigroup homomorphism
{\overline{\phi}:\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\to\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P^{\prime})}
such that the following diagram commutes (where both vertical maps are inclusions):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P^{\prime}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\phi}}

and moreover any
*
-semigroup homomorphism
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\to\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P^{\prime})
has the form
\overline{\phi}
for some projection algebra morphism
\phi:P\to P^{\prime}
. ∎

Remark 5.10
.

Let us pause briefly to illustrate the significance of coreflectivity in Theorems
5.1
and
5.2
,
by a comparison with the category
{\bf Sgp}
of semigroups and the set-based free objects within it.
The latter are the semigroups
X^{+}
, consisting of all words over a set
X
under the operation of concatenation.
The assignment
X\mapsto X^{+}
can be viewed as a functor
{{\bf F}^{\prime}:{\bf Set}\rightarrow{\bf Sgp}}
.
It is a left adjoint to the forgetful functor
{\bf U}^{\prime}:{\bf Sgp}\rightarrow{\bf Set}
, which maps any semigroup to its underlying set.
However,
{\bf U}^{\prime}{\bf F}^{\prime}
is not naturally isomorphic, let alone equal, to the identity on
{\bf Sgp}
: indeed,

{\bf U}^{\prime}{\bf F}^{\prime}(X)
is the underlying set of the free semigroup
X^{+}
.
On the level of morphisms this is manifested by the fact that not every morphism
X^{+}\rightarrow Y^{+}
arises from a mapping
X\rightarrow Y
.

Remark 5.11
.

Lawson in
[
45
, Theorem 2.2.4]
states that groups form a
reflective
subcategory of inverse semigroups. The
left
adjoint to the inclusion maps an inverse semigroup to its maximum group image (the quotient by the least congruence that identifies all idempotents).
On the other hand one can check from the definitions that semilattices (commutative idempotent semigroups) form a coreflective subcategory of inverse semigroups.
Similarly, the category of (regular) biordered sets is coreflective in the category of (regular) semigroups; see
[
52
, Theorem 3.40]
and
[
51
, Theorem 6.10]
. Coreflectivity of
{\bf PA}
in
{\bf RSS}
can be viewed as a ‘regular
*
-analogue’ of these last two facts.

Remark 5.12
.

There is another way to view the semigroups
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
as free objects. Namely, for any (fixed) projection algebra
P
, there is a category
{\bf RSS}(P)
with:

•

objects all regular
*
-semigroups with projection algebra
P
, and

•

morphisms all
*
-morphisms that map projections identically.

We see then that
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is an initial object in this category, meaning that for every object
S
of
{\bf RSS}(P)
there is exactly one morphism
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\to S
(in this category). This follows by applying Theorem
5.8
(ii)
to the identity morphism
\phi=\operatorname{id}_{P}:P\to P={\bf P}(S)
. Note that the image of the morphism
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\to S
is the projection-generated subsemigroup of
S
, which of course is equal to the idempotent-generated subsemigroup of
S
.

6
Projection algebras and biordered sets

We have just seen that the chain semigroups are the
{\bf P}
-free objects in
{\bf RSS}
, where
{{\bf P}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}}
is the forgetful functor that maps a regular
*
-semigroup
S
to its underlying projection algebra
{\bf P}(S)
. Another forgetful functor
{\bf E}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf RSBS}
has been considered (implicitly) in the literature
[
54
]
, where
{\bf RSBS}
denotes the category of
regular
*
-biordered sets
; this will be defined formally below.
It is then natural to ask whether this forgetful functor has an adjoint, and if so what the
{\bf E}
-free objects are. Perhaps more fundamentally, we would like to understand the relationship between the categories
{\bf PA}
and
{\bf RSBS}
. It turns out that these categories are in fact
equivalent
, as we show in Theorem
6.19
below. This then has the consequence that
{\bf E}
-free objects do indeed exist, but that they are the same as the
{\bf P}
-free objects.

To establish this equivalence, we need functors

{\bf E}:{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSBS}\and{\bf P}:{\bf RSBS}\to{\bf PA}

with natural isomorphisms
{\bf P}\circ{\bf E}\cong\operatorname{id}_{\bf PA}
and
{\bf E}\circ{\bf P}\cong\operatorname{id}_{\bf RSBS}
. These functors are constructed in Subsections
6.2
and
6.3
, and the category equivalence is established in Subsection
6.4
. A salient part of the argument is Proposition
6.10
, which shows that regular
*
-semigroups with the same projection algebra have isomorphic
*
-biordered sets.

6.1
Preliminaries on biordered sets

We begin with the necessary definitions; for more background, and proofs, see
[
51
, Chapter 1]
and
[
54
, Section 2]
.

The set
E={\bf E}(S)=\{e\in S:e^{2}=e\}
of all idempotents of a semigroup
S
can be given the structure of a partial algebra called a

]

, then

and

are both idempotents (at least one of which is equal to

or

). It follows that we can define a partial operation

on

with domain

(E,{\cdot})
is the
biordered set
of
S

boset
for short. Since the (partial) product in
E
is just a restriction of the (total) product in
S

There is an axiomatic definition of abstract bosets (with no reference to any over-semigroup), but we will not need to give that here, as it is known that any abstract boset is the boset of idempotents of some semigroup
[
24
]
.

We denote by
{\bf BS}
the category of bosets. Morphisms in
{\bf BS}
are called
bimorphisms
,
6
6

6

The term ‘bimorphism’ comes from Nambooripad’s original work
[
51
]
, as a contraction of ‘biordered set morphism’, and should not be confused with other uses of the term, for example to mean a bijective morphism of graphs.
and are simply morphisms of partial algebras, i.e. functions
\phi:E\to E^{\prime}
such that for all
e,f\in E
,

(e,f)\in\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}(E)\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt(e\phi,f\phi)\in\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}(E^{\prime})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt(ef)\phi=(e\phi)(f\phi).

(6.1)

An isomorphism in
{\bf BS}
will be called a
bisomorphism
; these are the bijections
\phi:E\to E^{\prime}
for which
\phi
and
\phi^{-1}
are both bimorphisms. It is easy to see that a bijection
\phi:E\to E^{\prime}
is a bisomorphism if and only if

\text{$\phi$ is a bimorphism}\and\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}(E^{\prime})=\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}(E)\phi=\{(e\phi,f\phi):(e,f)\in\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}(E)\}.

A boset is called
regular
if it is the boset of a regular semigroup. Such regular bosets can be abstractly characterised as the bosets
E

whose
sandwich sets

\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(e,f)
are non-empty for all
e,f\in E
. These sandwich sets are defined as follows. We begin by defining the set (of ‘mixed’ common lower bounds):

and
hf\mathrel{\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 0 L 19.69 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{2.27998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 3.15 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 3.32 C 2.35 1.33 1.18 0.39 0 0 C 1.18 -0.39 2.35 -1.33 2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}gf]
. The sandwich set is then

\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(e,f)=\{g\in\operatorname{\textup{{M}}}(e,f):h\preceq g\text{ for all }h\in\operatorname{\textup{{M}}}(e,f)\},

the set of all
\preceq
-maximum elements in
\operatorname{\textup{{M}}}(e,f)
. It turns out (see
[
51
, Theorem 1.1]
) that if
E
is a regular boset, and if
S
is
any
regular semigroup with
E={\bf E}(S)
, then

\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(e,f)=\{g\in E:egf=ef\text{ and }fge=g\text{ in }S\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any $e,f\in E$}.

(6.2)

Note here that the products
egf
and
ef
are taken in
S
, but need not be defined in
E
itself; these products need not even be idempotents.

We write
{\bf RBS}
for the category of regular bosets. Morphisms in
{\bf RBS}
are called
regular bimorphisms
, and are the bimorphisms
\phi:E\to E^{\prime}
(as above) that map sandwich sets into sandwich sets, meaning that

\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(e,f)\phi\subseteq\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(e\phi,f\phi)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $e,f\in E$.}

Regular bosets are isomorphic in
{\bf RBS}
if and only if they are isomorphic in
{\bf BS}
. This is because sandwich sets are defined directly from the (partial) ‘multiplication tables’ of bosets, and are hence preserved by bisomorphisms.

Following
[
54
]
, a
*
-boset
is a partial algebra
E=(E,{\cdot},{}^{*})
, where
(E,{\cdot})
is a boset, and
∗
is a unary operation
E\to E:e\mapsto e^{*}
satisfying the following, for all
e,f\in E
:

(SB1)

(e^{*})^{*}=e
,

(SB2)

(e,f)\in\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}(E)\ \Rightarrow\ (e^{*},f^{*})\in\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}(E)
and
(ef)^{*}=f^{*}e^{*}
.

We say
E=(E,{\cdot},{}^{*})
is a
regular
*
-boset
if
(E,\cdot)
is regular, and we additionally have:

(SB3)

For all
e\in E
, there exist elements
s=s(e)
and
t=t(e)
of
E
such that

e
s
t
e^{*}
,

and such that
e^{*}(gs)=(tg)e^{*}
for all
g\mathrel{\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 0 L 19.13 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{2.27998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 3.15 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 3.32 C 2.35 1.33 1.18 0.39 0 0 C 1.18 -0.39 2.35 -1.33 2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{14.02638pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 19.41 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}e
.

(We note that these were called ‘special
*
-biordered sets’ in
[
54
]
, where regular
*
-semigroups were also called ‘special
*
-semigroups’. Condition
(SB3)
is known as
\tau
-commutativity
, as it can be stated in terms of commutative diagrams involving natural maps between down-sets of idempotents in the poset
(E,{\mathrel{\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 0 L 19.13 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{2.27998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 3.15 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 3.32 C 2.35 1.33 1.18 0.39 0 0 C 1.18 -0.39 2.35 -1.33 2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{14.02638pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 19.41 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}})
.)

If
S
is a regular
*
-semigroup, then the boset
E={\bf E}(S)
becomes a regular
*
-boset whose unary operation is the restriction of the involution of
S
. The elements
s,t\in E
in
(SB3)
are
s=ee^{*}
and
t=e^{*}e
; for any
g\mathrel{\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 0 L 19.13 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{2.27998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 3.15 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 3.32 C 2.35 1.33 1.18 0.39 0 0 C 1.18 -0.39 2.35 -1.33 2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{14.02638pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 19.41 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}e
, the products
e^{*}(gs)
and
(tg)e^{*}
both evaluate to
e^{*}ge^{*}
.
Conversely, we have the following:

Theorem 6.3

(see
[
54
, Corollary 2.7]
)
.

Any regular
*
-boset is the
*
-boset of a regular
*
-semigroup. ∎

We denote by
{\bf RSBS}
the category of regular
*
-bosets. Morphisms in
{\bf RSBS}
are called
regular
*
-bimorphisms
, and are the regular bimorphisms
\phi:E\to E^{\prime}
(as above) that respect the involutions, meaning that

(e^{*})\phi=(e\phi)^{*}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $e\in E$.}

As above, regular
*
-bosets
E
and
E^{\prime}
are isomorphic in
{\bf RSBS}
if and only if there is a bisomorphism
E\to E^{\prime}
that also respects the involutions.

The assignment
S\mapsto{\bf E}(S)
is the object part of a (forgetful) functor

{\bf E}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf RSBS}.

(6.4)

A
*
-morphism
\phi:S\to S^{\prime}
in
{\bf RSS}
is sent to its restriction
{\bf E}(\phi)=\phi|_{{\bf E}(S)}:{\bf E}(S)\to{\bf E}(S^{\prime})
, which is a regular
*
-bimorphism in
{\bf RSBS}
.

6.2
From projection algebras to
*
-biordered sets

Theorem
6.19
below establishes a category equivalence
{\bf PA}\leftrightarrow{\bf RSBS}
. For this we need functors in both directions between the two categories. We can immediately obtain a functor

{\bf E}:{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSBS}

(6.5)

by composing the functors

{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSS}:P\mapsto\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\and{\bf RSS}\to{\bf RSBS}:S\mapsto{\bf E}(S)

from Theorem
5.1
and (
6.4
).
Note that the functors
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf RSBS}
and
{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSBS}
in (
6.4
) and (
6.5
) are both denoted by
{\bf E}
.
As these take different kinds of arguments (regular
*
-semigroups or projection algebras, and their morphisms), it will always be clear from context which one is meant.

The functor
{\bf E}:{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSBS}
in (
6.5
) maps a projection algebra
P
to the regular
*
-boset of
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
. Using
(CP5)
, we have

{\bf E}(P)={\bf E}(\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P))=\{[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]:(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}\}

(6.6)

as a set. We will describe the biordered structure of
{\bf E}(P)
in Proposition
6.9
below; the involution is of course given by
[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]^{*}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[q,p]\hskip-1.5649pt]
.

The functor
{\bf E}:{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSBS}
maps a projection algebra morphism
\phi:P\to P^{\prime}
to the regular
*
-bimorphism

{\bf E}(\phi)={\bf E}(\overline{\phi}):{\bf E}(P)\to{\bf E}(P^{\prime}),

where
\overline{\phi}:\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\to\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P^{\prime})
is the
*
-morphism from Theorem
5.9
. So
{\bf E}(\phi)
is the restriction of
\overline{\phi}
to
{\bf E}(P)={\bf E}(\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P))
; explicitly, we have
[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]{\bf E}(\phi)=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p\phi,q\phi]\hskip-1.5649pt]
for
(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
.

For the next two proofs, we note that the product of idempotents
e=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]
and
f=[\hskip-1.5649pt[r,s]\hskip-1.5649pt]
in
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is given by

e\bullet f=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime},s^{\prime}]\hskip-1.5649pt],\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003ptp^{\prime}=r\theta_{q}\theta_{p},\hskip 10.00002ptq^{\prime}=r\theta_{q},\hskip 10.00002ptr^{\prime}=q\theta_{r}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pts^{\prime}=q\theta_{r}\theta_{s}.

(6.7)

In particular, if
e\bullet f
happens to be an idempotent, then

e\bullet f=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p^{\prime},s^{\prime}]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[r\theta_{q}\theta_{p},q\theta_{r}\theta_{s}]\hskip-1.5649pt].

(6.8)

Proposition 6.9
.

If
P
is a projection algebra, and if
e=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]
and
f=[\hskip-1.5649pt[r,s]\hskip-1.5649pt]
, then

(i)

e\mathrel{\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 0 L 19.69 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{2.27998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 3.15 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 3.32 C 2.35 1.33 1.18 0.39 0 0 C 1.18 -0.39 2.35 -1.33 2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}f\textup{ (i.e.~$e=e\bullet f$)}\ \Leftrightarrow\ {\bf r}(e)\leq{\bf r}(f)\ \Leftrightarrow\ q\leq s
, in which case
f\bullet e=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p\theta_{s}\theta_{r},s\theta_{p}\theta_{q}]\hskip-1.5649pt]
,

(ii)

e\mathrel{\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 19.13 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{14.02638pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 19.41 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}f\textup{ (i.e.~$e=f\bullet e$)}\ \Leftrightarrow\ {\bf d}(e)\leq{\bf d}(f)\ \Leftrightarrow\ p\leq r
, in which case
e\bullet f=[\hskip-1.5649pt[r\theta_{q}\theta_{p},q\theta_{r}\theta_{s}]\hskip-1.5649pt]
.

Proof.

For the first part (the second is dual), it is enough to show that
e=e\bullet f\ \Leftrightarrow\ q\leq s
, as the expression for
f\bullet e
will then follow from (
6.8
). Write
e\bullet f=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime},s^{\prime}]\hskip-1.5649pt]
, as in (
6.7
).
If
e=e\bullet f
, then
q=s^{\prime}=q\theta_{r}\theta_{s}\leq s
.
Conversely, suppose
q\leq s
, so that
q=q\theta_{s}
. Combining this with
\theta_{s}=\theta_{s}\theta_{r}\theta_{s}
(which holds by
(PA5)
), we calculate

q=q\theta_{s}=q\theta_{s}\theta_{r}\theta_{s}=q\theta_{r}\theta_{s}=s^{\prime}.

From
q\leq s\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}r
it follows from
(PA2)
that
q\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}r
, and so
q=r\theta_{q}=q^{\prime}
. But also

p^{\prime}=r\theta_{q}\theta_{p}=q\theta_{p}=p,

and so
e\bullet f=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime},s^{\prime}]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q,r^{\prime},q]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]=e
, as required.
∎

The functor
{\bf E}:{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSBS}
constructs
{\bf E}(P)
from a projection algebra
P
by first passing through
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
. Perhaps surprisingly, it turns out that we could have passed through
any
regular
*
-semigroup
S
with projection algebra
P={\bf P}(S)
, and we would obtain the same
*
-boset up to isomorphism,
{\bf E}(S)\cong{\bf E}(P)
:

Proposition 6.10
.

If
S
is a regular
*
-semigroup with projection algebra
P={\bf P}(S)
, then the map

{\bf E}(P)\to{\bf E}(S):[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]\mapsto pq

is an isomorphism of
*
-bosets.

Proof.

As in Remark
5.12
, there exists a
*
-morphism
\Phi(=\overline{\operatorname{id}}_{P}):\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\to S
with
p\Phi=p
for all
p\in P
. Applying the functor
{\bf E}
from (
6.4
), we obtain the regular
*
-bimorphism

\phi={\bf E}(\Phi)=\Phi|_{{\bf E}(P)}:{\bf E}(P)\to{\bf E}(S),

which is the map from the statement.
As explained in Subsection
6.1
, and since we already know that
\phi
is a (regular)
*
-bimorphism, we can complete the proof that this is an isomorphism by checking that:

(i)

\phi
is a bijection, and

(ii)

\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}({\bf E}(P))\phi=\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}({\bf E}(S))
.

(i)
. This follows by applying
(RS7)
in both
S
and
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
, and keeping in mind
p\bullet q=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]
.

(ii)
. Since
\phi
is a bijection, this amounts to showing that

(e,f)\in\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}({\bf E}(P))\ \Leftrightarrow\ (e\phi,f\phi)\in\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}({\bf E}(S))\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $e,f\in{\bf E}(P)$.}

The forward implication is clear, as
\phi
is a bimorphism. Conversely, suppose
(e\phi,f\phi)\in\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}({\bf E}(S))
, where
e,f\in{\bf E}(P)
, and write
e=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]
and
f=[\hskip-1.5649pt[r,s]\hskip-1.5649pt]
. By symmetry we may assume that
{e\phi\mathrel{\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 0 L 19.69 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{2.27998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 3.15 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 3.32 C 2.35 1.33 1.18 0.39 0 0 C 1.18 -0.39 2.35 -1.33 2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}f\phi}
, i.e.
e\phi=(e\phi)(f\phi)
; we complete the proof by showing that
e\mathrel{\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 0 L 19.69 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{2.27998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 3.15 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 3.32 C 2.35 1.33 1.18 0.39 0 0 C 1.18 -0.39 2.35 -1.33 2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}f
, i.e.
{e=e\bullet f}
. To do so, first write
e\bullet f=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime},s^{\prime}]\hskip-1.5649pt]
, as in (
6.7
).
By Theorem
3.9
, the
*
-morphism
\Phi:\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\to S
is also a chained projection functor
{{\bf G}(\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P))=(P,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\nu)\to{\bf G}(S)=(P,\mathcal{G},\varepsilon)}
. Since
\Phi
is the identity on
P=v\overline{\mathscr{C}}=v\mathcal{G}
, it follows that
\Phi
is a
v
-functor
\overline{\mathscr{C}}\to\mathcal{G}
, and so

s^{\prime}={\bf r}(e\bullet f)={\bf r}((e\bullet f)\Phi)={\bf r}((e\Phi)(f\Phi))={\bf r}((e\phi)(f\phi))={\bf r}(e\phi)={\bf r}(e)=q.

Consequently,
q=s^{\prime}=q\theta_{r}\theta_{s}\leq s
, and we then obtain
e\mathrel{\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 0 L 19.69 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{2.27998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 3.15 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 3.32 C 2.35 1.33 1.18 0.39 0 0 C 1.18 -0.39 2.35 -1.33 2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}f
from Proposition
6.9
.
∎

Remark 6.11
.

Combining Theorem
6.3
and Proposition
6.10
, it follows that every regular
*
-boset is isomorphic to
{\bf E}(P)
for some projection algebra
P
.

Remark 6.12
.

Another approach to constructing a boset
{\bf E}(P)
from a projection algebra
P
would be to take the underlying set

{\bf E}(P)={\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}=\{(p,q)\in P\times P:p=q\theta_{p}\text{ and }q=p\theta_{q}\},

and define the
\mathrel{\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 0 L 19.69 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{2.27998pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 3.15 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.88 3.32 C 2.35 1.33 1.18 0.39 0 0 C 1.18 -0.39 2.35 -1.33 2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
and
\mathrel{\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 19.13 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{14.02638pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 19.41 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
pre-orders, and basic products, as in Proposition
6.9
. One would then need to check that the boset axioms are satisfied. Taking this approach, Proposition
6.10
would then state that
(p,q)\mapsto pq
is an isomorphism
{\bf E}(P)\to{\bf E}(S)
for any regular
*
-semigroup
S
with projection algebra
P={\bf P}(S)
.

6.3
From
*
-biordered sets to projection algebras

Now that we have constructed a functor
{\bf E}:{\bf PA}\to{\bf RSBS}
, we wish to construct a functor
{\bf P}:{\bf RSBS}\to{\bf PA}
in the reverse direction. (Again, this functor
{\bf RSBS}\to{\bf PA}
has the same name as the forgetful functor
{\bf P}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}
considered earlier.) This is somewhat more involved than the functor
{\bf E}
, which was simply the composition of two previously existing functors.

Consider a regular
*
-boset
E=(E,{\cdot},{}^{*})
. The underlying set of the projection algebra
{\bf P}(E)
is simply the set of fixed points under the involution,

{\bf P}(E)=\{p\in E:p=p^{*}\},

the elements of which are called the
projections
of
E
. To define the operations in
{\bf P}(E)
we need the following special case of
[
54
, Proposition 2.5]
. We give a simple adaptation of the proof for completeness.

Lemma 6.13
.

If
E
is a regular
*
-boset, and if
p,q\in{\bf P}(E)
, then there exists a unique element
e=e(p,q)
of the sandwich set
\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(p,q)
for which
pe,eq\in{\bf P}(E)
. Moreover, we have

e=qp\and pe=pqp

in any regular
*
-semigroup
S
with
*
-boset
E={\bf E}(S)
.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary regular
*
-semigroup
S
with
E={\bf E}(S)
.
We obtain
qp\in\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(p,q)
from (
6.2
) and
(RS2)
, and
p(qp),(qp)q\in{\bf P}(E)
from
(RS5)
.

For uniqueness, suppose
e\in\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(p,q)
is such that
pe,eq\in{\bf P}(E)
. We first claim that

pqp=pe\and qpq=eq.

(6.14)

We prove the first, and the second is analogous. Define the projections
s=pqp
and
t=pe
. Using
(RS2)
and (
6.2
) in the indicated places, we calculate

s=\underline{pq}p=\underline{pe}qp=tqp\ \Rightarrow\ s=ts\and t=p\underline{e}=\underline{pq}ep=\underline{pqp}qep=sqep\ \Rightarrow\ t=st.

Since
s
and
t
are projections it then follows that
s=s^{*}=(ts)^{*}=s^{*}t^{*}=st=t
, as claimed. Combining
(RS2)
, (
6.2
) and (
6.14
), we obtain

e=\underline{e}e=e\underline{pe}=\underline{ep}qp=\underline{eq}p=\underline{qpqp}=qp.\qed

From now on we fix the notation
e(p,q)
from Lemma
6.13
. It is important to note that while the products
qp
and
pqp
in this lemma are taken in the semigroup
S
, the products
pe
and
eq
exist in the boset
E
itself.

Lemma 6.15
.

If
\phi:E\to E^{\prime}
is a regular
*
-bimorphism, then
e(p,q)\phi=e(p\phi,q\phi)
for all
p,q\in{\bf P}(E)
.

Proof.

With
e=e(p,q)
, Lemma
6.13
gives
e\in\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(p,q)
and
pe,eq\in{\bf P}(E)
. We deduce
{e\phi\in\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(p\phi,q\phi)}
from regularity of
\phi
, and
(p\phi)(e\phi),(e\phi)(q\phi)\in{\bf P}(E^{\prime})
because
\phi
is a
*
-bimorphism. It then follows from uniqueness in Lemma
6.13
that
e\phi=e(p\phi,q\phi)
.
∎

Definition 6.16
.

For a regular
*
-boset
E
, we define
{\bf P}(E)
to be the projection algebra with underlying set
{\bf P}(E)=\{p\in E:p=p^{*}\}
, and operations given by

q\theta_{p}=pe(p,q)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $p,q\in{\bf P}(E)$.}

This is well defined by Lemma
6.13
, which also tells us that
{\bf P}(E)={\bf P}(S)
is the projection algebra of any regular
*
-semigroup
S
with
*
-boset
E={\bf E}(S)
.

For a regular
*
-bimorphism
{\phi:E\to E^{\prime}}
, we define
{\bf P}(\phi)
to be the restriction

{\bf P}(\phi)=\phi|_{{\bf P}(E)}:{\bf P}(E)\to{\bf P}(E^{\prime}).

(6.17)

(Note that
\phi
maps projections to projections because it is a
*
-bimorphism.)

Proposition 6.18
.

{\bf P}
is a functor
{\bf RSBS}\to{\bf PA}
.

Proof.

To show that
{\bf P}(\phi):{\bf P}(E)\to{\bf P}(E^{\prime})
as in (
6.17
) is a projection algebra morphism, let
{p,q\in{\bf P}(E)}
. We then use Lemma
6.15
to calculate

(q\theta_{p})\phi=(pe(p,q))\phi=(p\phi)(e(p,q)\phi)=(p\phi)e(p\phi,q\phi)=(q\phi)\theta_{p\phi}.

It is again clear that the laws
{\bf P}(\phi\circ\phi^{\prime})={\bf P}(\phi)\circ{\bf P}(\phi^{\prime})
and
{\bf P}(\operatorname{id}_{E})=\operatorname{id}_{{\bf P}(E)}
hold.
∎

6.4
A category equivalence, and more on freeness

We now establish the promised category equivalence.

Theorem 6.19
.

We have

{\bf P}\circ{\bf E}=\operatorname{id}_{\bf PA}\and{\bf E}\circ{\bf P}\cong\operatorname{id}_{\bf RSBS}.

Consequently, the functors
{\bf P}
and
{\bf E}
furnish an equivalence of the categories
{\bf PA}
and
{\bf RSBS}
.

Proof.

To show that
{\bf P}\circ{\bf E}=\operatorname{id}_{\bf PA}
we need to show that

•

{\bf P}({\bf E}(P))=P
for any projection algebra
P
, and

•

{\bf P}({\bf E}(\phi))=\phi
for any projection algebra morphism
\phi:P\to P^{\prime}
.

Since
{\bf E}(\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P))={\bf E}(P)
, it follows from Definition
6.16
that
{\bf P}({\bf E}(P))={\bf P}(\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P))=P
. For the statement concerning
\phi
, we follow the definitions to compute

{\bf P}({\bf E}(\phi))={\bf E}(\phi)|_{{\bf P}({\bf E}(P))}={\bf E}(\phi)|_{P}=\phi.

To show that
{\bf E}\circ{\bf P}\cong\operatorname{id}_{\bf RSBS}
, we will construct a natural isomorphism
\eta:{\bf E}\circ{\bf P}\to\operatorname{id}_{\bf RSBS}
. Towards this, we claim that for any regular
*
-boset
E
, the map

\eta_{E}:{\bf E}({\bf P}(E))\to E:[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]\mapsto e(q,p)

is a
*
-bisomorphism. To see this, let
S
be a regular
*
-semigroup with
*
-boset
E={\bf E}(S)
, and write
P={\bf P}(E)={\bf P}(S)
. Since
pq=e(q,p)
in
S
by Lemma
6.13
, it follows that
\eta_{E}
is precisely the isomorphism from Proposition
6.10
.

We now show that
\eta=(\eta_{E})
is a natural isomorphism
{{\bf E}\circ{\bf P}\to\operatorname{id}_{\bf RSBS}}
. Since each
\eta_{E}
is an isomorphism, it remains to check that for any regular
*
-bimorphism
\phi:E\to E^{\prime}
the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf E}\circ{\bf P}(E)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf E}\circ{\bf P}(E^{\prime})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bf E}\circ{\bf P}(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

But the elements of
{\bf E}\circ{\bf P}(E)
have the form
[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]
for
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related projections
p,q\in{\bf P}(E)
,
and we use Lemma
6.15
to calculate

[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\ \eta_{E}\ }e(q,p)\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\ \phi\ }e(q\phi,p\phi)\and[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\ {\bf E}\circ{\bf P}(\phi)\ }[\hskip-1.5649pt[p\phi,q\phi]\hskip-1.5649pt]\mapstochar\xrightarrow{\ \eta_{E^{\prime}}\ }e(q\phi,p\phi).\qed

Combining Theorems
5.1
and
6.19
, we obtain the following:

Theorem 6.20
.

The functor
{\bf RSBS}\to{\bf RSS}:E\mapsto\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}({\bf P}(E))
is a left adjoint to the forgetful functor
{\bf RSS}\to{\bf RSBS}:S\mapsto{\bf E}(S)
, and
{\bf RSBS}
is coreflective in
{\bf RSS}
. ∎

It follows that the chain semigroups are the free objects in
{\bf RSS}
with respect to both forgetful functors

{\bf P}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf PA}:S\mapsto{\bf P}(S)\and{\bf E}:{\bf RSS}\to{\bf RSBS}:S\mapsto{\bf E}(S).

The regular
*
-semigroups
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}({\bf P}(E))
are defined in terms of the regular
*
-boset
E
. Analogously to
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
and
\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)
, we denote this by

\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}^{*}(E)=\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}({\bf P}(E)).

Note that it is possible for regular
*
-bosets
E
and
E^{\prime}
to have different underlying sets, but have identical
projection algebras
{\bf P}(E)={\bf P}(E^{\prime})
.
This would occur if
E
and
E^{\prime}
were isomorphic, with different underlying sets, but with the same set of projections.
In this case we would of course have
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}^{*}(E)=\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}^{*}(E^{\prime})
.
This means that the assignment
E\mapsto\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}^{*}(E)
is not injective.

One could get around this ‘problem’ by instead defining
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}^{*}(E)
to be a copy of
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}({\bf P}(E))
in which we identify each
e\in E
with
[\hskip-1.5649pt[ee^{*},e^{*}e]\hskip-1.5649pt]\in\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}({\bf P}(E))
.
This would then reflect the situation of projection algebras, where
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)=\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P^{\prime})\ \Leftrightarrow\ P=P^{\prime}
, as follows from Theorem
4.22
.

7
Presentations

In this section we establish a number of presentations for the free (projection-generated) regular
*
-semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
over an arbitrary projection algebra
P
. The first presentation (Theorem
7.2
) involves the generating set
P
. The second and third (Theorems
7.10
and
7.13
) are both in terms of the generating set
E={\bf E}(P)
, and these highlight the connections between
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
and the free (idempotent-generated) semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
and the free regular (idempotent-generated) semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)
.

7.1
Preliminaries on presentations

We begin by establishing the notation we will use for presentations; for more details, see for example
[
37
, Section 1.6]
. We also prove a general technical lemma that will be used in this section and later in the paper.

A
congruence
on a semigroup
S
is an equivalence relation
\sigma
on
S
that is compatible with multiplication, in the sense that
a\mathrel{\sigma}a^{\prime}
and
b\mathrel{\sigma}b^{\prime}
together imply
ab\mathrel{\sigma}a^{\prime}b^{\prime}
. The quotient
S/\sigma
is then a semigroup under the induced operation on
\sigma
-classes. Given a semigroup homomorphism
\phi:S\to T
, the
kernel

\ker(\phi)=\{(a,b)\in S\times S:a\phi=b\phi\}
is a congruence on
S
, and the fundamental homomorphism theorem for semigroups states that
S/{\ker(\phi)}\cong\operatorname{im}(\phi)
.

For a set
X
, we denote by
X^{+}
the free semigroup over
X
, which consists of all non-empty words over
X
, under concatenation. For a set
R\subseteq X^{+}\times X^{+}
of pairs of words, we write
R^{\sharp}
for the congruence on
X^{+}
generated by
R
, i.e. the least congruence on
X^{+}
containing
R
. We often write
[w]_{R}
for the
R^{\sharp}
-class of
w\in X^{+}
. We say a semigroup
S
has
presentation

\langle X:R\rangle
if
S\cong X^{+}/R^{\sharp}
, i.e. if there is a surjective semigroup homomorphism
X^{+}\to S
with kernel
R^{\sharp}
. At times we identify
\langle X:R\rangle
with the semigroup
X^{+}/R^{\sharp}
itself. The elements of
X
and
R
are called generators and (defining) relations, respectively. A relation
(u,v)\in R
is typically displayed as an equality:
u=v
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Consider semigroups
S=\langle X:R\rangle
and
T=\langle Y:Q\rangle
, for which the following hold:

(i)

X\subseteq Y
,

(ii)

R\subseteq Q^{\sharp}
,

(iii)

every
y\in Y
is
Q^{\sharp}
-equivalent to a word over
X
,

(iv)

there is a morphism
\phi:Y^{+}\to S
with
Q\subseteq\ker(\phi)
, and
x\phi=[x]_{R}
for all
x\in X
.

Then
S\cong T
.

Proof.

By
(i)
we have a well defined morphism
\psi:X^{+}\to T:x\mapsto[x]_{Q}
.
By
(ii)
and
(iv)
,
\psi
and
\phi
induce morphisms

\Psi:S\to T:[x]_{R}\mapsto[x]_{Q}\and\Phi:T\to S:[y]_{Q}\mapsto y\phi.

By
(iii)
,
T
is generated by
\{[x]_{Q}:x\in X\}
, and of course
S
is generated by
\{[x]_{R}:x\in X\}
. Since

[x]_{R}\Psi=[x]_{Q}\and[x]_{Q}\Phi=x\phi=[x]_{R}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $x\in X$, by \ref{lempres4},}

it follows that
\Psi
and
\Phi

are mutually inverse isomorphisms of
S
and
T
.
∎

7.2
Presentation over
P

Here is the main result of this section:

Theorem 7.2
.

For any projection algebra
P
, the free regular
*
-semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
has presentation

\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\cong\langle X_{P}:R_{P}\rangle,

where
X_{P}=\{x_{p}:p\in P\}
is an alphabet in one-one correspondence with
P
, and where
R_{P}
is the set of relations

\displaystyle x_{p}^{2}

\displaystyle=x_{p}

for all
p\in P
,

(R1)

\displaystyle(x_{p}x_{q})^{2}

\displaystyle=x_{p}x_{q}

for all
p,q\in P
,

(R2)

\displaystyle x_{p}x_{q}x_{p}

\displaystyle=x_{q\theta_{p}}

for all
p,q\in P
.

(R3)

Remark 7.3
.

If
P={\bf P}(S)
is the projection algebra of a regular
*
-semigroup
S
, then recall that
q\theta_{p}=pqp
for
p,q\in P
, where the product
pqp
is taken in
S
. Thus, relations of type
(R3)
have the form
x_{p}x_{q}x_{p}=x_{pqp}
in this case.

To prove Theorem
7.2
, we require some technical lemmas. But first, it is worth observing that relations
(R1)
–
(R3)
closely resemble projection algebra axioms
(P2)
,
(P4)
and
(P5)
, i.e. those that are stated purely in terms of the
\theta
maps.

For the rest of this subsection, we fix
P
,
X_{P}
and
R_{P}
as in Theorem
7.2
.
We write
{\sim}=R_{P}^{\sharp}
for the congruence on
X_{P}^{+}
generated by relations
(R1)
–
(R3)
, and use
\sim_{1}
to indicate equivalence by one or more applications of
(R1)
, and similarly for
\sim_{2}
and
\sim_{3}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

If
p,q\in P
are such that
q\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}p
, then
x_{p}x_{q}\sim x_{p^{\prime}}x_{q}
for some
p^{\prime}\in P
with
q\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p^{\prime}\leq p
.

Proof.

Let
p^{\prime}=q\theta_{p}\leq p
. Combining
q\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}p
with
(PA1)
, it follows that
q=p\theta_{q}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q\theta_{p}=p^{\prime}
. We also have

x_{p}x_{q}\sim_{2}x_{p}x_{q}x_{p}x_{q}\sim_{3}x_{q\theta_{p}}x_{q}=x_{p^{\prime}}x_{q}.\qed

Lemma 7.5
.

For any
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\in P
we have
x_{p_{1}}\cdots x_{p_{k}}\sim x_{p_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots x_{p_{k}^{\prime}}
for some
p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{k}^{\prime}\in P
with
p_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}\cdots\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{k}^{\prime}
and
p_{i}^{\prime}\leq p_{i}
for all
i
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by induction on
k
. The
k=1
case being trivial, we assume
k\geq 2
. With
p_{1}^{\prime\prime}=p_{2}\theta_{p_{1}}\leq p_{1}
and
p_{2}^{\prime\prime}=p_{1}\theta_{p_{2}}\leq p_{2}
we have

x_{p_{1}}x_{p_{2}}\sim_{2}x_{p_{1}}x_{p_{2}}x_{p_{1}}x_{p_{2}}x_{p_{1}}x_{p_{2}}\sim_{3}x_{p_{2}\theta_{p_{1}}}x_{p_{1}\theta_{p_{2}}}=x_{p_{1}^{\prime\prime}}x_{p_{2}^{\prime\prime}},

and
(PA1)
gives
p_{1}^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{2}^{\prime\prime}
. By induction, we have

x_{p_{2}^{\prime\prime}}x_{p_{3}}\cdots x_{p_{k}}\sim x_{p_{2}^{\prime}}x_{p_{3}^{\prime}}\cdots x_{p_{k}^{\prime}}

for some
p_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{k}^{\prime}\in P
with
p_{2}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}\cdots\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{k}^{\prime}
,
p_{2}^{\prime}\leq p_{2}^{\prime\prime}
and
p_{i}^{\prime}\leq p_{i}
for
i=3,\ldots,k
. Note that also
p_{2}^{\prime}\leq p_{2}^{\prime\prime}\leq p_{2}
.
Since
p_{2}^{\prime}\leq p_{2}^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{1}^{\prime\prime}
,
(PA2)
gives
p_{2}^{\prime}\leq_{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}p_{1}^{\prime\prime}
. It then follows from Lemma
7.4
that
x_{p_{1}^{\prime\prime}}x_{p_{2}^{\prime}}\sim x_{p_{1}^{\prime}}x_{p_{2}^{\prime}}
for some
p_{1}^{\prime}\in P
with
p_{2}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{1}^{\prime}\leq p_{1}^{\prime\prime}
, and again we observe that
p_{1}^{\prime}\leq p_{1}^{\prime\prime}\leq p_{1}
. Putting everything together we have

x_{p_{1}}x_{p_{2}}x_{p_{3}}\cdots x_{p_{k}}\sim x_{p_{1}^{\prime\prime}}x_{p_{2}^{\prime\prime}}x_{p_{3}}\cdots x_{p_{k}}\sim x_{p_{1}^{\prime\prime}}x_{p_{2}^{\prime}}x_{p_{3}^{\prime}}\cdots x_{p_{k}^{\prime}}\sim x_{p_{1}^{\prime}}x_{p_{2}^{\prime}}x_{p_{3}^{\prime}}\cdots x_{p_{k}^{\prime}},

with all conditions met.
∎

Given a
P
-path
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\in\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(P)
, we define the word

w_{\mathfrak{p}}=x_{p_{1}}\cdots x_{p_{k}}\in X_{P}^{+}.

It follows from Lemma
7.5
that every word over
X_{P}
is
\sim
-equivalent to some
w_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Using
(R1)
, it is easy to see that

w_{\mathfrak{p}}w_{\mathfrak{q}}\sim w_{\mathfrak{p}\circ\mathfrak{q}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for any $\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\in\mathscr{P}$ with ${\bf r}(\mathfrak{p})={\bf d}(\mathfrak{q})$.}

(7.6)

The next result refers to the congruence
{\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}}=\Xi^{\sharp}
on
\mathscr{P}
from Definition
4.7
.

Lemma 7.7
.

For any
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\in\mathscr{P}
, we have
\mathfrak{p}\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}\mathfrak{q}\ \Rightarrow\ w_{\mathfrak{p}}\sim w_{\mathfrak{q}}
.

Proof.

It suffices to assume that
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{q}
differ by a single application of
(

Ω

1)
–
(

Ω

3)
, i.e. that

\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\circ\mathfrak{s}\circ\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime}\and\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\circ\mathfrak{t}\circ\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for some $\mathfrak{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{P}$ and $(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\Xi\cup\Xi^{-1}$.}

Since
w_{\mathfrak{p}}\sim w_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}w_{\mathfrak{s}}w_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime}}
and
w_{\mathfrak{q}}\sim w_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}w_{\mathfrak{t}}w_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime}}
by (
7.6
), it is in fact enough to prove that

w_{\mathfrak{s}}\sim w_{\mathfrak{t}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\Xi$.}

We consider the three forms the pair
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\Xi
can take.

(

Ω

1)
. This follows immediately from
(R1)
.

(

Ω

2)
. If
\mathfrak{s}=(p,q,p)
and
\mathfrak{t}=(p)
for some
(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
, then
w_{\mathfrak{s}}=x_{p}x_{q}x_{p}\sim_{3}x_{q\theta_{p}}=x_{p}=w_{\mathfrak{t}}
.

(

Ω

3)
. Finally, suppose
\mathfrak{s}=\lambda(e,p,f)=(e,e\theta_{p},f)
and
\mathfrak{t}=\rho(e,p,f)=(e,f\theta_{p},f)
for some
p\in P
, and some
p
-linked pair
(e,f)
. Then

w_{\mathfrak{s}}=x_{e}x_{e\theta_{p}}x_{f}\sim_{3}x_{e}x_{p}x_{e}x_{p}x_{f}\sim_{2}x_{e}x_{p}x_{f}\sim_{2}x_{e}x_{p}x_{f}x_{p}x_{f}\sim_{3}x_{e}x_{f\theta_{p}}x_{f}=w_{\mathfrak{t}}.\qed

We can now tie together the loose ends.

Proof of Theorem
7.2
.

Define the homomorphism

\Psi:X_{P}^{+}\to\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{by}\hskip 20.00003ptx_{p}\Psi=p=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p]\hskip-1.5649pt]\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for $p\in P$.}

To see that
\Psi
is surjective, let
\mathfrak{c}\in\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
, so that
\mathfrak{c}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]
for some
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\in\mathscr{P}
.
We claim that

[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{i}]\hskip-1.5649pt]=p_{1}\bullet\cdots\bullet p_{i}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all $1\leq i\leq k$.}

Indeed, the
i=1
case is clear, and if
2\leq i\leq k
then

\displaystyle p_{1}\bullet\cdots\bullet p_{i}

\displaystyle=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{i-1}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\bullet p_{i}

by induction

\displaystyle=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{i-1}]\hskip-1.5649pt]{\downharpoonright}_{p_{i-1}^{\prime}}\circ[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{i-1}^{\prime},p_{i}^{\prime}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\circ{}_{p_{i}^{\prime}}{\downharpoonleft}p_{i}

where
p_{i-1}^{\prime}=p_{i}\theta_{p_{i-1}}
and
p_{i}^{\prime}=p_{i-1}\theta_{p_{i}}

\displaystyle=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{i-1}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\circ[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{i-1},p_{i}]\hskip-1.5649pt]\circ p_{i}

since
p_{i-1}^{\prime}=p_{i-1}
and
p_{i}^{\prime}=p_{i}
, as
p_{i-1}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}p_{i}

\displaystyle=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{i-1},p_{i}]\hskip-1.5649pt],

proving the claim. It then follows that

\mathfrak{c}=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}]\hskip-1.5649pt]=p_{1}\bullet\cdots\bullet p_{k}=(x_{p_{1}}\cdots x_{p_{k}})\Psi=w_{\mathfrak{p}}\Psi,

(7.8)

completing the proof that
\Psi
is surjective.

Next, we note that
R_{P}\subseteq\ker(\Psi)
, meaning that
u\Psi=v\Psi
(in
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
) for all
(u,v)\in R_{P}
. Indeed, this is clear when
(u,v)
has type
(R1)
, and follows from
(RS2)
or
(CP4)
for type
(R2)
or
(R3)
.

It remains to show that
\ker(\Psi)\subseteq R_{P}^{\sharp}
. To do so, fix some
(u,v)\in\ker(\Psi)
, so that
u,v\in X_{P}^{+}
and
u\Psi=v\Psi
; we must show that
u\sim v
(recall that we write
\sim
for
R_{P}^{\sharp}
). By Lemma
7.5
, we have
u\sim w_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
v\sim w_{\mathfrak{q}}
for some
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\in\mathscr{P}
. Using (
7.8
), and remembering that
{\sim}\subseteq\ker(\Psi)
, we have

[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]=w_{\mathfrak{p}}\Psi=u\Psi=v\Psi=w_{\mathfrak{q}}\Psi=[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{q}]\hskip-1.5649pt],

meaning that
\mathfrak{p}\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}\mathfrak{q}
. But then
w_{\mathfrak{p}}\sim w_{\mathfrak{q}}
by Lemma
7.7
, so
u\sim w_{\mathfrak{p}}\sim w_{\mathfrak{q}}\sim v
, as required.
∎

7.3
Presentation as a quotient of
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)

Consider a boset
E
, and recall that a product
ef
is defined in
E
precisely when
(e,f)\in\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}(E)
is a basic pair, i.e. when
\{ef,fe\}\cap\{e,f\}\not=\varnothing
. The
free (idempotent-generated) semigroup over
E
has presentation

\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)=\langle X_{E}:x_{e}x_{f}=x_{ef}\ \text{for all $(e,f)\in\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}(E)$}\rangle,

(7.9)

where here
X_{E}=\{x_{e}:e\in E\}
is an alphabet in one-one correspondence with
E
. The boset of
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
is isomorphic to
E

[
24
]
, and consists of all equivalence classes of letters
x_{e}
(
e\in E
).

Now consider a projection algebra
P
, and let
E={\bf E}(P)={\bf E}(\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P))
be the (regular
*
-) boset of
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
. Since
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is a semigroup with biordered set
E={\bf E}(P)
, general theory
[
24
, Theorem 3.3]
tells us that the mapping
x_{e}\mapsto e
(
e\in E
) induces a surmorphism
{\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)\to\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)}
. Thus, we know in advance that there exists a presentation for
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
extending the above presentation for
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
by means of additional relations. Theorem
7.10
below gives an explicit such presentation, with additional relations
x_{p}x_{q}=x_{pq}
for projections
p,q\in P(\subseteq E)
.
These additional relations can also be viewed as a generating set for the kernel of the surmomorphism
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)\to\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
.
Note that the product
pq
might not exist in the boset
E
, but it certainly exists in the semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
, and is an idempotent, and hence a well-defined element of
E
; in fact,
pq
is the element
e(q,p)
from Lemma
6.13
.
For simplicity, we will denote the product in
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
by juxtaposition instead of
\bullet
throughout this subsection.

Theorem 7.10
.

For any projection algebra
P
, the free regular
*
-semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
has presentation

\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\cong\langle X_{E}:R_{E}\rangle,

where
X_{E}=\{x_{e}:e\in E\}
is an alphabet in one-one correspondence with
E={\bf E}(P)
, and where
R_{E}
is the set of relations

\displaystyle x_{e}x_{f}

\displaystyle=x_{ef}

for all
(e,f)\in\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}(E)
,

(R1)
′

\displaystyle x_{p}x_{q}

\displaystyle=x_{pq}

for all
p,q\in P
.

(R2)
′

Proof.

By Theorem
7.2
we have
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\cong\langle X_{P}:R_{P}\rangle
. Thus, we can prove the current theorem by applying Lemma
7.1
with
S=\langle X_{P}:R_{P}\rangle
and
{T=\langle X_{E}:R_{E}\rangle}
