. To do so, we must show that:

(i)

X_{P}\subseteq X_{E}
,

(ii)

R_{P}\subseteq R_{E}^{\sharp}
,

(iii)

every
x_{e}
(
e\in E
) is
\sim^{\prime}
-equivalent to a word over
X_{P}
, where
{\sim^{\prime}}=R_{E}^{\sharp}
, and

(iv)

there is a morphism
\phi:X_{E}^{+}\to\langle X_{P}:R_{P}\rangle
such that
R_{E}\subseteq\ker(\phi)
, and
x_{p}\phi=[x_{p}]
for all
p\in P
, where we write
[w]
for the
R_{P}^{\sharp}
-class of
w\in X_{P}^{+}
.

Item
(i)
is clear. For
(ii)
, we check the relations from
R_{P}
in turn. We use
\sim^{\prime}_{1}
and
\sim^{\prime}_{2}
to denote equivalence via
(R1)′
or
(R2)′
.

(R1)
. This is contained in
(R1)′
(and in
(R2)′
).

(R2)
. If
p,q\in P
, then
x_{p}x_{q}\sim^{\prime}_{2}x_{pq}\sim^{\prime}_{1}x_{pq}^{2}\sim^{\prime}_{2}(x_{p}x_{q})^{2}
.

(R3)
. If
p,q\in P
, then
pq\in E
, and
(p,pq)
is a basic pair in
E
. It follows that
(R1)′
contains the relation
x_{pq}x_{p}=x_{pqp}
. But then
x_{p}x_{q}x_{p}\sim^{\prime}_{2}x_{pq}x_{p}\sim^{\prime}_{1}x_{pqp}=x_{q\theta_{p}}
.

For
(iii)
, fix some
e\in E
. Since
e=ee^{*}e^{*}e
, with
ee^{*},e^{*}e\in P
, we have

x_{e}=x_{ee^{*}e^{*}e}\sim^{\prime}_{2}x_{ee^{*}}x_{e^{*}e}\in X_{P}^{+}.

For
(iv)
, we first define a morphism

\psi:X_{E}^{+}\to\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P):x_{e}\mapsto e.

To see that
R_{E}\subseteq\ker(\psi)
, fix some
e,f\in E
. If
ef\in E
, then
\psi
maps both
x_{e}x_{f}
and
x_{ef}
to
ef
, and it follows that relations
(R1)′
and
(R2)′
are both preserved. Composing
\psi:X_{E}^{+}\to\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
with the isomorphism
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\to\langle X_{P}:R_{P}\rangle:p\mapsto[x_{p}]
gives
\phi:X_{E}^{+}\to\langle X_{P}:R_{P}\rangle
with the required properties.
∎

Remark 7.11
.

Relations
(R2)′
reflect the fact that the product of two projections is always an idempotent in a regular
*
-semigroup. In fact, any idempotent is the product of two
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related projections by
(RS3)
.
Consequently, one might wonder if we could replace
(R2)′
by the subset consisting of the relations

x_{p}x_{q}=x_{pq}
for
(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
,
and still define
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
.
It turns out that this is not possible in general. For example, consider the three element semilattice
S=\{0,e,f\}
with
ef=0
, and note that
P=E=S
and
{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}=\Delta_{P}
in this case.
As
(R2)′
is a copy of the multiplication table of
S
(which is the case for any semilattice), it follows that
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\cong S
.
Since
{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}=\Delta_{P}
, the relations in
(R2)′
arising from friendly pairs are just
x_{p}^{2}=x_{p}
for
p\in E
, which are already contained in
(R1)′
. So if we reduce the presentation in this way,
we actually arrive at the free idempotent-generated semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
.
As shown in
[
10
, Example 2]
,
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
is infinite (and non-regular), and so certainly not isomorphic to
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
.
In the next subsection we will see that if instead of
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
we start with a presentation for
\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)
, then the relations from
(R2)′
arising from friendly pairs are indeed sufficient to define
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
.

Remark 7.12
.

Consider a projection algebra
P
, and its associated boset
E={\bf E}(P)
. We have now given presentations for
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
in terms of (copies of) the generating sets
P
(Theorem
7.2
) and
E
(Theorem
7.10
). On the other hand,
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
is defined in terms of a presentation with generating set
E
(see (
7.9
)), and one might wonder if there is a presentation utilising the generating set
P
. This is not the case, however, as
P
need not generate
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
in general.
We will give a concrete instance of this in Example
8.1
.

7.4
Presentation as a quotient of
\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)

Consider again a projection algebra
P
, and its associated boset
{E={\bf E}(P)={\bf E}(\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P))}
. Since
E
is regular (i.e. the boset of a regular semigroup, namely
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
), we also have the
free regular (idempotent-generated) semigroup

\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)
. This was defined by Nambooripad in
[
51
]
using his groupoid machinery, and in
[
56
]
by means of the presentation

\displaystyle\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)=\langle X_{E}:{}

\displaystyle x_{e}x_{f}=x_{ef}\ \ \ \hskip 18.49411pt\text{for all $(e,f)\in\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}(E)$,}

\displaystyle x_{e}x_{g}x_{f}=x_{e}x_{f}\ \ \ \text{for all $e,f\in E$ and $g\in\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(e,f)$}\rangle.

Here
\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(e,f)
is the sandwich set of the idempotents
e,f\in E
, defined in Subsection
6.1
. Note that the characterisation of
\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(e,f)
in (
6.2
) applies to
S=\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
, as
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is a regular semigroup with boset
E
.
The above presentation shows that
\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)
is a quotient of
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
. In turn, our next result shows that
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is a quotient of
\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)
, with the additional relations generating the kernel of the canonical surmorphism
\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)\to\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
.

Theorem 7.13
.

For any projection algebra
P
, the free regular
*
-semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
has presentation

\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\cong\langle X_{E}:R_{E}^{\prime}\rangle,

where
X_{E}=\{x_{e}:e\in E\}
is an alphabet in one-one correspondence with
E={\bf E}(P)
, and where
R_{E}^{\prime}
is the set of relations

\displaystyle x_{e}x_{f}

\displaystyle=x_{ef}

for all
(e,f)\in\operatorname{\textup{{BP}}}(E)
,

(R1)
′′

\displaystyle x_{e}x_{f}

\displaystyle=x_{e}x_{g}x_{f}

for all
e,f\in E
and
g\in S(e,f)
,

(R2)
′′

\displaystyle x_{p}x_{q}

\displaystyle=x_{pq}

for all
(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}
.

(R3)
′′

Proof.

We begin with the presentation
\langle X_{E}:R_{E}\rangle=\langle X_{E}:\text{\ref{R1'},\ \ref{R2'}}\rangle
from Theorem
7.10
, via the mapping

\psi:X_{E}^{+}\to\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P):x_{e}\mapsto e.

Since
\psi
maps
x_{e}x_{g}x_{f}
and
x_{e}x_{f}
both to
egf=ef
for
g\in\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(e,f)
, we can add relations
(R2)′′
to the presentation. Noting that
(R1)′
and
(R1)′′
are the same sets of relations, the presentation has now become

\langle X_{E}:\text{\ref{R1''},\ \ref{R2''},\ \ref{R2'}}\rangle.

Since
(R3)′′
is contained in
(R2)′
, we can complete the proof by showing that each relation in
(R2)′
is implied by those in
R_{E}^{\prime}
. To do so, let
p,q\in P
be arbitrary, and let
p^{\prime}=q\theta_{p}
and
q^{\prime}=p\theta_{q}
, so that
p^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}q^{\prime}
and
p^{\prime}q^{\prime}=pq
. We then calculate (again writing
\sim_{1}^{\prime\prime}
for equivalence by
(R1)′′
, and so on)

\displaystyle x_{p}x_{q}

\displaystyle\sim^{\prime\prime}_{2}x_{p}x_{qp}x_{q}

as
qp\in\operatorname{\textup{{S}}}(p,q)

\displaystyle\sim^{\prime\prime}_{1}x_{p}x_{qp}x_{qp}x_{q}

\displaystyle\sim^{\prime\prime}_{1}x_{p\cdot qp}x_{qp\cdot q}

as
(qp,p)
and
(q,qp)
are basic pairs

\displaystyle=x_{p^{\prime}}x_{q^{\prime}}\sim^{\prime\prime}_{3}x_{p^{\prime}q^{\prime}}=x_{pq}.

8
Adjacency semigroups and bridging path semigroups

We now turn to explicit examples of free projection-generated regular
*
-semigroups, starting with those arising from adjacency semigroups, as introduced in Subsection
2.2
.

Let
\Gamma=(P,E)
be a symmetric, reflexive digraph, and let
A_{\Gamma}
be its adjacency semigroup.
We keep the notation of Subsection
2.2
, including the projection algebra
P_{0}=P\cup\{0\}
, whose operations were given in (
2.2
). We now consider the structure of the free regular
*
-semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P_{0})
.

The path category
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}(P_{0})
consists of tuples of the form
(0,\ldots,0)
, and
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
where each
(p_{i},p_{i+1})\in E(\subseteq{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}})
;
the latter are simply the paths in
\Gamma
in the usual graph-theoretical sense, with repeated vertices allowed as in Subsection
3.3
.

As in Remark
3.6
, any non-zero path
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
is
\approx
-equivalent to a unique
reduced
path
\overline{\mathfrak{p}}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
, where each
p_{i}
is distinct from
p_{i+1}
(if
i\leq k-1
) and from
p_{i+2}
(if
i\leq k-2
). By identifying a non-zero chain (
\approx
-class of a path) with the unique reduced path it contains, we may identify the chain groupoid
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P_{0})
with the set of reduced paths in
\Gamma
, along with
0=[0]
.

Using (
2.2
), we see that
(e,f)
is
p
-linked if and only if
e=f=0
or
(e,p)
and
(p,f)
are both edges of
\Gamma
. In these respective cases, we have

\lambda(e,p,f)=\rho(e,p,f)=(0,0,0)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{or}\hskip 20.00003pt\lambda(e,p,f)=\rho(e,p,f)=(e,p,f).

It follows that the congruences
\approx
and
\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}
are equal, and so
\overline{\mathscr{C}}=\mathscr{C}
, and
[\hskip-1.5649pt[\mathfrak{p}]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\mathfrak{p}]
for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{P}
.

The free regular
*
-semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P_{0})
can therefore be viewed as follows:

•

The elements are
0
and the reduced paths in
\Gamma
.

•

The product of reduced paths
\mathfrak{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
and
\mathfrak{q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{l})
is given by

\mathfrak{p}\bullet\mathfrak{q}=\begin{cases}\overline{\mathfrak{p}\oplus\mathfrak{q}}&\text{if $(p_{k},q_{1})\in E$ is an edge of $\Gamma$}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Here
\oplus
denotes concatenation, so
\mathfrak{p}\oplus\mathfrak{q}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},q_{1},\ldots,q_{l})
.

•

The involution is given by reversal of paths.

•

The non-zero projections are the empty paths
p=(p)
, which are in one-one correspondence with the vertices of
\Gamma
.

•

The remaining non-zero idempotents are the non-loop edges
p\bullet q=(p,q)
of
\Gamma
.

The authors have not seen these specific semigroups in the literature, but we note that they are closely related to the
graph inverse semigroups
introduced in
[
2
]
, in which
\mathfrak{p}\bullet\mathfrak{q}
is only non-zero when
p_{k}=q_{1}
; there are some other differences as well, including the fact that the digraphs of
[
2
]
are not assumed to be symmetric or reflexive.
In our case
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{q}
can also be composed if there is an edge
p_{k}\to q_{1}
, which ‘bridges’ the end of
\mathfrak{p}
and the start of
\mathfrak{q}
. We therefore call
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}({\bf P}(A_{\Gamma}))
the
bridging path semigroup of
\Gamma
, and denote it by
B_{\Gamma}
.
Note that bridging path semigroups can be defined starting from an arbitrary digraph, i.e. without assuming symmetry and reflexivity a priori. The properties of such a semigroup would then depend on the properties of the graph
\Gamma
, and this may be an interesting direction for study. In particular, one can verify that
B_{\Gamma}
is a regular
*
-semigroup with projections
P_{0}
and the involution given by reversal of paths if and only if
\Gamma
is symmetric and reflexive.

In the special case that
\Gamma
is the complete digraph, the adjacency semigroup
A_{\Gamma}
is simply the square band
B_{P}=P\times P
with a zero adjoined. This band
B_{P}
is a regular
*
-semigroup, with operations
(p,q)(r,s)=(p,s)
and
(p,q)^{*}=(q,p)
. Every element of
B_{P}
is an idempotent; the projections are
p=(p,p)
; and the projection algebra
{\bf P}(B_{P})=P
has a trivial structure, in the sense that the
\theta_{p}
operations are all constant maps.
The above analysis shows that the semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
consists of all reduced tuples, with product
\mathfrak{p}\bullet\mathfrak{q}=\overline{\mathfrak{p}\oplus\mathfrak{q}}
.
In the next two examples we give some more explicit details concerning the square bands
B_{P}
, and the corresponding free regular
*
-semigroups
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
, in the cases that
|P|\leq 3
.

Example 8.1
.

If
|P|\leq 2
then
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)=B_{P}
is finite. By contrast, the corresponding free idempotent-generated semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
is infinite when
|P|=2
; this is folklore and can be deduced from
[
33
, Theorem 5]
. Here we write
E=B_{P}={\bf E}(B_{P})\cong{\bf E}(P)
.
More specifically, it follows from from
[
33
, Theorem 5]
that
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
is isomorphic to the
2\times 2
Rees matrix semigroup
S
over an infinite cyclic group
H=\langle a\rangle
, with respect to the sandwich matrix
\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
1&a\end{smallmatrix}\big)
. In particular, this lets us prove the claim from Remark
7.12
, namely that
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
is not generated by (its canonical copy of)
P
.

To see this, write
P=\{p,q\}
. The above-mentioned isomorphism
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)\to S
maps (the equivalence classes of) the letters
x_{p}
and
x_{q}
to the idempotents
\overline{p}=(1,1,1)
and
\overline{q}=(2,a^{-1},2)
, respectively. (The other two idempotents of
S
are
(1,1,2)
and
(2,1,1)
.) It is easy to see that every element of
\langle\overline{p},\overline{q}\rangle
has the form
(i,a^{-m},j)
for some
i,j\in\{1,2\}
and
m\geq 0
, so indeed
\langle\overline{p},\overline{q}\rangle\not=S
.

Example 8.2
.

If
|P|\geq 3
then
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is infinite, and in particular
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\not=B_{P}
.
It is instructive to consider the case where
P=\{p,q,r\}
has size
3
. For
s,t\in P
, let
H_{s,t}
be the set of all reduced paths from
s
to
t
, so that
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)=\bigsqcup_{s,t\in P}H_{s,t}
. Note for example that

H_{p,p}=\{p\}\cup\{(p,q,r,p)^{k},(p,r,q,p)^{k}:k=1,2,\ldots\}.

It is easy to check that
H_{p,p}
is isomorphic to the infinite cyclic group
H=\langle a\rangle
. The identity of
H_{p,p}
is
p
, and
(p,q,r,p)^{k}
and
(p,r,q,p)^{k}
are inverses of each other. An analogous argument shows that each
H_{s,t}\cong H
. It follows from general structure theory (see
[
37
, Chapter 3]
) that
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is a
3\times 3
Rees matrix semigroup over
H
, with respect to the sandwich matrix

\biggl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&1\\
1&1&a\\
1&a^{-1}&1\end{smallmatrix}\biggr)
.
By way of comparison,
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}({\bf E}(B_{P}))=\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}({\bf E}(B_{P}))
is a
3\times 3
Rees matrix semigroup over the free group of rank
4
, again following from
[
33
, Theorem 5]
.

The structure of an arbitrary bridging path semigroup
B_{\Gamma}=\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}({\bf P}(A_{\Gamma}))
can be similarly described in terms of Rees
0
-matrix semigroups over free groups. The description requires an analysis of the maximal subgroups of free regular
*
-semigroups, which is the subject of the forthcoming paper
[
29
]
.

Our final example in this section is an extension
P^{\prime}
of the projection algebra
P
of the
3\times 3
rectangular band from Example
8.2
by a single extra projection. It was introduced in
[
26
]
(where it had an identity adjoined), and was originally discovered by Michael Kinyon.
We note that Kinyon’s projection algebra is not the projection algebra of an adjacency semigroup. It illustrates the fact that it is possible for a projection algebra
P
with
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
infinite to be contained in a projection algebra
P^{\prime}
with
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P^{\prime})
finite.

Example 8.3
.

Let
P^{\prime}=\{p,q,r,e\}
be the projection algebra with operations

\theta_{p}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p&q&r&e\\
p&p&p&p\end{smallmatrix}\right),\hskip 20.00003pt\theta_{q}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p&q&r&e\\
q&q&q&q\end{smallmatrix}\right),\hskip 20.00003pt\theta_{r}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p&q&r&e\\
r&r&r&r\end{smallmatrix}\right)\and\theta_{e}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p&q&r&e\\
p&q&q&e\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Note that
p,q,r
are all
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related, but
e
is
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related only to itself. As in Example
8.2
, it follows that

\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P^{\prime})=\{e\}\cup\bigsqcup_{s,t\in\{p,q,r\}}H_{s,t}.

However,
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
is a proper quotient of
\mathscr{C}
here, as there is a non-trivial linked pair. Specifically,
(p,r)
is
e
-linked, and we have

\lambda(p,e,r)=(p,p\theta_{e},r)=(p,p,r)\and\rho(p,e,r)=(p,r\theta_{e},r)=(p,q,r).

Consequently,
[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q,r]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,p,r]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,r]\hskip-1.5649pt]
in
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P^{\prime})
. It follows from this that
[\hskip-1.5649pt[s,t,u]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[s,u]\hskip-1.5649pt]
for distinct
s,t,u\in\{p,q,r\}
. For example,

[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,r,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q,r,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[p,q]\hskip-1.5649pt]\and[\hskip-1.5649pt[q,p,r]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[q,p,q,r]\hskip-1.5649pt]=[\hskip-1.5649pt[q,r]\hskip-1.5649pt].

The other three cases are obtained by inverting the three already considered. This all shows that
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P^{\prime})
contains exactly ten elements:

•

the projections
e,p,q,r
, and

•

the remaining idempotents
s\bullet t=[\hskip-1.5649pt[s,t]\hskip-1.5649pt]
, for distinct
s,t\in\{p,q,r\}
.

The entire multiplication table of
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P^{\prime})
can be obtained from the fact that
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P^{\prime})\setminus\{e\}=\langle p,q,r\rangle
is a
3\times 3
rectangular band, together with the rules

p\bullet e=p,\hskip 20.00003ptq\bullet e=q\and r\bullet e=[\hskip-1.5649pt[r,q]\hskip-1.5649pt].

9
Temperley–Lieb monoids

In Section
8
we saw examples of naturally occurring (albeit very small) regular
*
-semigroups
S
that were isomorphic to their associated free regular
*
-semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}({\bf P}(S))
.
In this section, we present a decidedly non-trivial family of semigroups displaying this phenomenon, namely the Temperley–Lieb monoids, introduced in Subsection
2.3
.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
P={\bf P}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})
be the projection algebra of a finite Temperley–Lieb monoid
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
. Then
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\cong\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
.

Proof.

We begin with the presentations
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}\cong\langle X_{T}:R_{T}\rangle
and
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\cong\langle X_{P}:R_{P}\rangle
from Theorems
2.3
and
7.2
, and apply Lemma
7.1
to show that they are equivalent. For this, we first need to convert the monoid presentation
\langle X_{T}:R_{T}\rangle
into a semigroup presentation
\langle X_{T}^{\prime}:R_{T}^{\prime}\rangle
with
X_{T}^{\prime}\subseteq X_{P}
, which we do as follows.
First, we identify each generator
t_{i}\in X_{T}
with
x_{\tau_{i}}\in X_{P}
, noting that they both represent the element
\tau_{i}
of
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
; see (
2.4
).
Then we define
X_{T}^{\prime}=\{e\}\cup X_{T}
, where here
e=x_{1}
represents the identity
1
of
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
.
Finally we add new relations that ensure
e
acts as the identity. The resulting set
R_{T}^{\prime}
of relations is:

\displaystyle t_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=t_{i}

for all
i

(T1)

\displaystyle t_{i}t_{j}

\displaystyle=t_{j}t_{i}

if
|i-j|>1

(T2)

\displaystyle t_{i}t_{j}t_{i}

\displaystyle=t_{i}

if
|i-j|=1

(T3)

\displaystyle e^{2}

\displaystyle=e

(T4)

\displaystyle et_{i}=t_{i}e

\displaystyle=t_{i}

for all
i
.

(T5)

It is clear that
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}\cong\langle X_{T}^{\prime}:R_{T}^{\prime}\rangle
. We now work towards applying Lemma
7.1
, with
{S=\langle X_{T}^{\prime}:R_{T}^{\prime}\rangle}
and
T=\langle X_{P}:R_{P}\rangle
. For this we need to show that:

(i)

X_{T}^{\prime}\subseteq X_{P}
,

(ii)

R_{T}^{\prime}\subseteq R_{P}^{\sharp}
,

(iii)

every
x_{p}
(
p\in P
) is
\sim
-equivalent to a word over
X_{T}^{\prime}
, where again
{\sim}=R_{P}^{\sharp}
, and

(iv)

there is a morphism
\phi:X_{P}^{+}\to\langle X_{T}^{\prime}:R_{T}^{\prime}\rangle
such that
R_{P}\subseteq\ker(\phi)
, and
x\phi=[x]_{R_{T}^{\prime}}
for all
x\in X_{T}^{\prime}
.

We have already noted that
(i)
holds. For
(ii)
, we consider the relations from
R
in turn. Again we write
\sim_{1}
to denote equivalence via
(R1)
, and so on.

(T1)
and
(T4)
. These are contained in
(R1)
.

(T2)
. Fix
i,j
with
|i-j|>1
, and define the projection
p=\tau_{i}\tau_{j}\tau_{i}=\tau_{j}\tau_{i}\tau_{j}(=\tau_{i}\tau_{j})
. Then

t_{i}t_{j}=x_{\tau_{i}}x_{\tau_{j}}\sim_{2}x_{\tau_{i}}x_{\tau_{j}}x_{\tau_{i}}x_{\tau_{j}}x_{\tau_{i}}x_{\tau_{j}}\sim_{3}x_{\tau_{i}\tau_{j}\tau_{i}}x_{\tau_{j}\tau_{i}\tau_{j}}=x_{p}x_{p}\sim_{1}x_{p}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 10.00002ptt_{j}t_{i}\sim x_{p}.

(T3)
. We have
t_{i}t_{j}t_{i}=x_{\tau_{i}}x_{\tau_{j}}x_{\tau_{i}}\sim_{3}x_{\tau_{i}\tau_{j}\tau_{i}}=x_{\tau_{i}}=t_{i}
.

(T5)
. Writing
p=\tau_{i}
for simplicity, we have

et_{i}=x_{1}x_{p}\sim_{2}x_{1}x_{p}x_{1}x_{p}\sim_{3}x_{1p1}x_{p}=x_{p}x_{p}\sim_{1}x_{p}=t_{i}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and similarly}\hskip 20.00003ptt_{i}e\sim t_{i}.

For
(iii)
we first note that
x_{1}=e\in X_{T}^{\prime}
. For
p\not=1
we have
p=\tau_{i_{1}}\cdots\tau_{i_{k}}
(in
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
) for some
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}
, and then

p=p^{*}p=\tau_{i_{k}}\cdots\tau_{i_{1}}\tau_{i_{1}}\cdots\tau_{i_{k}}=\tau_{i_{k}}\cdots\tau_{i_{2}}\tau_{i_{1}}\tau_{i_{2}}\cdots\tau_{i_{k}}=\tau_{i_{1}}\theta_{\tau_{i_{2}}}\cdots\theta_{\tau_{i_{k}}}.

It follows that
x_{p}=x_{\tau_{i_{1}}\theta_{\tau_{i_{2}}}\cdots\theta_{\tau_{i_{k}}}}\sim_{3}x_{\tau_{i_{k}}}\cdots x_{\tau_{i_{2}}}x_{\tau_{i_{1}}}x_{\tau_{i_{2}}}\cdots x_{\tau_{i_{k}}}=t_{i_{k}}\cdots t_{i_{2}}t_{i_{1}}t_{i_{2}}\cdots t_{i_{k}}
.

Finally, for
(iv)
we begin by defining a morphism

\psi:X_{P}^{+}\to\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}:x_{p}\mapsto p.

We then have
R_{P}\subseteq\ker(\psi)
since
P={\bf P}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})
. We then obtain the desired
\phi:X_{P}^{+}\to\langle X_{T}^{\prime}:R_{T}^{\prime}\rangle
by composing
\psi:X_{P}^{+}\to\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
with the canonical isomorphism
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}\to\langle X_{T}^{\prime}:R_{T}^{\prime}\rangle
.
∎

It is natural to wonder about the relationships between other diagram monoids and their associated free projection-generated regular
*
-semigroups. These relationships tend to be more complicated than the case of
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
, as glimpsed in Example
10.15
below, and treated in detail for the partition monoid
\mathcal{P}_{n}
in the forthcoming paper
[
29
]
.

10
Topological interpretation

In this section we provide an alternative, topological interpretation of the groupoids
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)
and
\overline{\mathscr{C}}=\overline{\mathscr{C}}(P)
associated to a projection algebra
P
. Specifically, we can view
\mathscr{C}
as the fundamental groupoid of a natural graph
G_{P}
built from the
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-relation of
P
, and
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
as the fundamental groupoid of either of two
2
-complexes
K_{P}
and
K_{P}^{\prime}
. These complexes both have
G_{P}
as their
1
-skeleton, and their
2
-cells are induced by linked pairs of projections. The complex
K_{P}
is defined in terms of all linked pairs, and
K_{P}^{\prime}
in terms of a special subset that generates the others; in particular
K_{P}^{\prime}
is simplicial. Maximal subgroups of the semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
coincide up to isomorphism with fundamental groups of either of these two complexes.

10.1
Special and degenerate linked pairs

Consider a
p
-linked pair
(e,f)
in a projection algebra
P
. The projections
e,f,e\theta_{p},f\theta_{p}
(which are used to define the paths
\lambda(e,p,f)
and
\rho(e,p,f)
) need not all be distinct, and an important special case occurs when
e=e\theta_{p}
or
f=f\theta_{p}
(i.e. when
e\leq p
or
f\leq p
). In this case, we say
(e,f)
is a
special
p
-linked pair
.

Let
\Xi^{\prime}
be the subset of
\Xi
consisting of all pairs
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\mathscr{P}\times\mathscr{P}
of the form
(

Ω

1)
and
(

Ω

2)
, as well as:

(
\mathsf{\Omega}
3)
′

\mathfrak{s}=\lambda(e,p,f)
and
\mathfrak{t}=\rho(e,p,f)
, for some
p\in P
, and some special
p
-linked pair
(e,f)
,

and let
{\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}^{\prime}}=\Xi^{\prime\sharp}
be the congruence on
\mathscr{P}
generated by
\Xi^{\prime}
.

Proposition 10.1
.

We have
{\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}^{\prime}}={\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}}
.

Proof.

We just need to show that
\mathfrak{s}\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}^{\prime}\mathfrak{t}
whenever
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})
is a pair of type
(

Ω

3)
. So suppose
\mathfrak{s}=\lambda(e,p,f)
and
\mathfrak{t}=\rho(e,p,f)
for some
p
-linked pair
(e,f)
, and let
e^{\prime}=e\theta_{p}
and
f^{\prime}=f\theta_{p}
. Then one can show that
(e,f^{\prime})
and
(e^{\prime},f)
are special
p
-linked pairs (with
f^{\prime}\leq p
in the first, and
e^{\prime}\leq p
in the second), and we have

\displaystyle\lambda(e,p,f^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(e,e^{\prime},f^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\lambda(e^{\prime},p,f)

\displaystyle=(e^{\prime},e^{\prime},f)\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}^{\prime}(e^{\prime},f),

\displaystyle\rho(e,p,f^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(e,f^{\prime},f^{\prime})\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}^{\prime}(e,f^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\rho(e^{\prime},p,f)

\displaystyle=(e^{\prime},f^{\prime},f).

Since
\Xi^{\prime}
contains the pairs
(\lambda(e,p,f^{\prime}),\rho(e,p,f^{\prime}))
and
(\lambda(e^{\prime},p,f),\rho(e^{\prime},p,f))
, it follows that
(e,e^{\prime},f^{\prime})\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}^{\prime}(e,f^{\prime})
and
(e^{\prime},f)\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}^{\prime}(e^{\prime},f^{\prime},f)
. But then

\mathfrak{s}=\lambda(e,p,f)=(e,e^{\prime},f)\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}^{\prime}(e,e^{\prime},f^{\prime},f)\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}^{\prime}(e,f^{\prime},f)=\rho(e,p,f)=\mathfrak{t}.\qed

Another case in which we do not need to include a pair
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})
of type
(

Ω

3)
is when we already have
\mathfrak{s}\approx\mathfrak{t}
.

Definition 10.6
.

We say a
p
-linked pair
(e,f)
is
degenerate
if
\lambda(e,p,f)\approx\rho(e,p,f)
.

The next result characterises such degenerate pairs at the level of the projection algebra structure.

Proposition 10.7
.

A
p
-linked pair
(e,f)
is degenerate if and only if
e\theta_{p}=f\theta_{p}
or
e,f\leq p
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof we write
e^{\prime}=e\theta_{p}
and
f^{\prime}=f\theta_{p}
, and also
\lambda=\lambda(e,p,f)=(e,e^{\prime},f)
and
\rho=\rho(e,p,f)=(e,f^{\prime},f)
.

(
\Leftarrow
). If
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}
, then
\lambda=\rho
. If
e,f\leq p
, then
e=e^{\prime}
and
f=f^{\prime}
, so that

\lambda=(e,e,f)\approx(e,f)\approx(e,f,f)=\rho.

(
\Rightarrow
). Suppose
\lambda\approx\rho
. If
\lambda
and
\rho
are reduced (in the sense of Remark
3.6
), then we must have
\lambda=\rho
, which implies
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}
. So now suppose
\lambda
and
\rho
are not reduced. In particular,
e\not=f
; cf.
(

Ω

2)
. For
\lambda
not to be reduced, it must then be the case that
e^{\prime}\in\{e,f\}
, and similarly
f^{\prime}\in\{e,f\}
. If
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}
then we are done. Otherwise,
\{e,f\}=\{e^{\prime},f^{\prime}\}=\{e\theta_{p},f\theta_{p}\}
, and it follows that
e,f\leq p
.
∎

It follows from Propositions
10.1
and
10.7
that the congruence
\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\approx$\crcr\kern-3.61664pt\cr$\approx$\crcr}}}}
on
\mathscr{P}
is generated by the set
\Xi^{\prime\prime}
of all pairs
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t})\in\mathscr{P}\times\mathscr{P}
of the form
(

Ω

1)
and
(

Ω

2)
, as well as:

(
\mathsf{\Omega}
3)
′′

\mathfrak{s}=\lambda(e,p,f)
and
\mathfrak{t}=\rho(e,p,f)
, for some
p\in P
, and some non-degenerate special
p
-linked pair
(e,f)
.

Remark 10.8
.

Consider a non-degenerate
p
-linked pair
(e,f)
, and write
e^{\prime}=e\theta_{p}
and
f^{\prime}=f\theta_{p}
, and also
\lambda=\lambda(e,p,f)=(e,e^{\prime},f)
and
\rho=\rho(e,p,f)=(e,f^{\prime},f)
. By Proposition
10.7
there are three possibilities:

(i)

\{e,f,e^{\prime},f^{\prime}\}
has size
4
,

(ii)

\{e,f,e^{\prime},f^{\prime}\}
has size
3
and
e\leq p
(i.e.
e=e^{\prime}
),

(iii)

\{e,f,e^{\prime},f^{\prime}\}
has size
3
and
f\leq p
(i.e.
f=f^{\prime}
),

with
(e,f)
being special in the second and third.
In case
(i)
, identification of the paths
\lambda
and
\rho
(cf.
(

Ω

3)
) amounts to commutativity of the diamond in Figure
4
(a), in the groupoid
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
.
In case
(ii)
, we have
\lambda\approx(e,f)
, so equating
\lambda
and
\rho
amounts to commutativity of the triangle in Figure
4
(b). Case
(iii)
corresponds to Figure
4
(c). Considering again case
(i)
, the proof of Proposition
10.1
showed that
(e,f^{\prime})
and
(e^{\prime},f)
are special
p
-linked pairs; since
e^{\prime},f^{\prime}\leq p
, these are of types
(iii)
and
(ii)
, respectively. This means that the two triangles commute in Figure
4
(d); commutativity of these triangles of course implies commutativity of the outer diamond in the same diagram, as per the final line of the proof of Proposition
10.1
.

On the other hand, a degenerate
p
-linked pair
(e,f)
leads to one of diagrams (e) or (f) in Figure
4
, in the cases
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}
and
e,f\leq p
, respectively. These diagrams already commute in
\mathscr{C}
, and we note that (e) and (f) picture the generic case in which all projections displayed are distinct (it is possible to have even more collapse).

Figure 4:
Diamonds, triangles and degeneracy of linked pairs of projections; see Remark
10.8
.

10.2
Graphs and complexes

We are now in a position to give the promised topological interpretation of the chain and reduced chain groupoids
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}(P)
and
\overline{\mathscr{C}}=\overline{\mathscr{C}}(P)
.
We begin by defining a graph
G_{P}
with:

•

vertex set
P
(a given projection algebra), and

•

an (undirected) edge
\{p,q\}
for every pair of distinct
\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}
-related projections
(p,q)\in{\mathrel{\mathscr{F}}}\setminus\Delta_{P}
.

We then define two
2
-complexes,
K_{P}
and
K_{P}^{\prime}
. These both have
G_{P}
as their
1
-skeleton.

•

The complex
K_{P}
has a
2
-cell with boundary
(e,e^{\prime},f,f^{\prime},e)
for every
p
-linked pair
(e,f)
, where as usual we write
e^{\prime}=e\theta_{p}
and
f^{\prime}=f\theta_{p}
.

•

The complex
K_{P}^{\prime}
is a sub-complex of
K_{P}
, and contains only the
2
-cells corresponding to non-degenerate special
p
-linked pairs
(e,f)
. Such a cell has boundary
(e,f,f^{\prime},e)
or
(e,e^{\prime},f,e)
when the pair is of type
(ii)
or
(iii)
, as enumerated in Remark
10.8
.

In particular, all cells in
K_{P}^{\prime}
are triangles.

The following result is essentially the observation that our categories
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{P}/\Omega^{\sharp}
and
{\overline{\mathscr{C}}=\mathscr{P}/\Xi^{\sharp}=\mathscr{P}/\Xi^{\prime\prime\sharp}}
are constructed in precisely the same way as the fundamental groupoids of the above graph and complexes. See for example
[
36
, Section 6]
.

Theorem 10.9
.

For any projection algebra
P
we have

\mathscr{C}(P)\cong\pi_{1}(G_{P})\and\overline{\mathscr{C}}\cong\pi_{1}(K_{P})\cong\pi_{1}(K_{P}^{\prime}),

as (unordered) groupoids. ∎

This has an immediate important consequence concerning the subgroups of the free regular
*
-semigroups
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
.
It follows from general semigroup theory that in an arbitrary semigroup
S
, maximal subgroups
are precisely the
\mathrel{\mathscr{H}}
-classes of idempotents; see
[
14
, Exercise 2.3.1]
.
The relation
\mathrel{\mathscr{H}}
is one of the five Green’s equivalences (see
[
37
, Section 2.1]
), but we do not require its actual definition, only that the
\mathrel{\mathscr{H}}
-class of
an idempotent
e
has the form

H_{e}=\{s\in S:se=es=s\text{ and }st=ts=e\text{ for some }t\in S\}.

If
e
and
f
are
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
-equivalent idempotents, which here can be simply taken to mean
e\mathrel{\hbox to14.63pt{\vbox to5.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-2.59998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.55 0 L 19.13 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{0.2pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 0.28 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{14.02638pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 19.41 0)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}f
using the notation introduced in Subsection
6.1
, then
H_{e}\cong H_{f}

[
37
, Proposition 2.3.6]
.
Specialising to the case where
S
is a regular
*
-semigroup, in which every idempotent
e
is
\mathrel{\mathscr{R}}
-related to the projection
ee^{*}

[
55
, Theorem 2.2]
, we see that
every maximal subgroup of
S
is isomorphic to one of the form
H_{p}
with
p\in{\bf P}(S)
, and that

H_{p}=\{s\in S\colon ss^{*}=s^{*}s=p\}
.
Finally, in the special case that
S=\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
for a projection algebra
P
, we see from (
4.20
) that the multiplication in
S
restricted to
H_{p}
is precisely the same as the composition in the reduced chain groupoid
\overline{\mathscr{C}}=\overline{\mathscr{C}}(P)
restricted to
\overline{\mathscr{C}}(p,p)
. Combining this with Theorem
10.9
, we obtain the following as a corollary:

Theorem 10.10
.

Let
P
be a projection algebra. Every maximal subgroup of
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is isomorphic to a fundamental group of
K_{P}
, or (equivalently) of
K_{P}^{\prime}
. Specifically, the
\mathrel{\mathscr{H}}
-class of any projection
p\in P
is isomorphic to
\pi_{1}(K_{P},p)\cong\pi_{1}(K_{P}^{\prime},p)
. ∎

We conclude this section and the paper by recasting the examples from Sections
8
and
9
in the terminology of this section, and adding two new ones. As always, the reader should have at the back of their mind the category isomorphism between regular
*
-semigroups and chained projection groupoids (Subsection
3.5
), and that under this isomorphism the free regular
*
-semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
and the reduced chain groupoid
\overline{\mathscr{C}}(P)
correspond to each other (Section
4
). The exposition will freely move between these two structures.

Example 10.11
.

Let
\Gamma=(P,E)
be a symmetric, reflexive digraph,
A_{\Gamma}
the adjacency semigroup,
P_{0}
its projection algebra, and
B_{\Gamma}=\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P_{0})
the bridging path semigroup, as in Subsection
2.2
and Section
8
. The graph
G_{P_{0}}
is the simple, undirected reduct of
\Gamma
with
0
added as a new, isolated vertex. As we saw in Section
8
, all linked pairs in
P_{0}
are degenerate, and so
K_{P_{0}}=K_{P_{0}}^{\prime}=G_{P_{0}}
. In particular,
\mathscr{C}=\overline{\mathscr{C}}
can be realised as the fundamental groupoid of the graph
G_{P_{0}}
,
and Theorem
10.10
implies that all maximal subgroups of
B_{\Gamma}=\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P_{0})
are free. This gives another way to see why
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is finite in Example
8.1
and infinite in Example
8.2
.

Example 10.12
.

Let
P^{\prime}=\{p,q,r,e\}
be the projection algebra from Example
8.3
. The graph
G_{P^{\prime}}
consists of a triangle with vertices
p,q,r
, and an isolated vertex
e
. The complex
K_{P^{\prime}}=K_{P^{\prime}}^{\prime}
has a
2
-cell attached to the triangle in
G_{P^{\prime}}
, coming from the
e
-linked pair
(p,r)
.

Example 10.13
.

In Theorem
9.1
, we showed that when
P={\bf P}(\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n})
is the projection algebra of the Temperley–Lieb monoid
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
, the free regular
*
-semigroup
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
.
This has a topological consequence.
The monoid
\mathcal{T}\!\mathcal{L}_{n}
is known to have no non-trivial subgroups; equivalently, it is
\mathrel{\mathscr{H}}
-trivial
[
58
]
.
Hence, all the fundamental groups of the complexes
K_{P}
and
K_{P}^{\prime}
are trivial, i.e. their connected components are simply connected.
It does not seem obvious, a priori, that this ought to be the case.

In the final two examples we use the topological viewpoint to gain some insight into the free regular
*
-semigroups
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}({\bf P}(\mathcal{M}_{n}))
associated to the Motzkin monoids
\mathcal{M}_{n}
, as defined in Subsection
2.3
, and the relationship to the idempotent-generated subsemigroups
\langle E(\mathcal{M}_{n})\rangle
, which were studied in
[
20
]
.

Example 10.14
.

Consider the Motzkin monoid
\mathcal{M}_{3}
, and let
P={\bf P}(\mathcal{M}_{3})
be the projection algebra of this monoid. The elements of
\mathcal{M}_{3}
are shown in Figure
5
in a so-called
egg-box diagram
(see
[
14
]
for more details), in which the idempotents are shaded; the projections are indicated by darker shading. The complex
K_{P}^{\prime}
is shown in Figure
6
. The connected component of
K_{P}^{\prime}
at the bottom of the figure contains three triangular
2
-cells, which are indicated by shading. (The outer triangle of this component is not the boundary of a
2
-cell.) To see for example that the ‘upper’ triangle is a
2
-cell, denote its vertices by

. Then with

, one can check that
(e,f)
is a special
p
-linked pair, with
e\theta_{p}=e
and
f\theta_{p}=g
.

It follows from this that the connected components of
K_{P}^{\prime}
are simply connected, and hence that
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is
\mathrel{\mathscr{H}}
-trivial. It follows that
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is isomorphic to its image under the natural morphism
\overline{\operatorname{id}}_{P}:\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)\to\mathcal{M}_{3}
from Theorem
5.8
. This image is the idempotent-generated subsemigroup
\langle{\bf E}(\mathcal{M}_{3})\rangle
.

Figure 5:
The elements of the Motzkin monoid
\mathcal{M}_{3}
; see Example
10.14
.

Figure 6:
The complex
K_{P}^{\prime}
, where
P={\bf P}(\mathcal{M}_{3})
is the projection algebra of the Motzkin monoid
\mathcal{M}_{3}
; see Example
10.14
.

One may wonder whether the same holds for larger Motzkin monoids, but this is not the case, as our final example shows.

Example 10.15
.

Let
P={\bf P}(\mathcal{M}_{4})
be the projection algebra of the Motzkin monoid
\mathcal{M}_{4}
. The complex
K_{P}^{\prime}
has
35
vertices, and eleven connected components, one of which is shown in Figure
7
; its six
2
-cells are shaded. It is apparent that the fundamental group(oid) of this component is infinite; specifically, a loop around the central square has infinite order. Consequently,
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
is infinite, and hence not isomorphic to
\langle{\bf E}(\mathcal{M}_{4})\rangle
.

Figure 7:
A connected component of the complex
K_{P}^{\prime}
, where
P={\bf P}(\mathcal{M}_{4})
is the projection algebra of the Motzkin monoid
\mathcal{M}_{4}
; see Example
10.15
.

Maximal subgroups of free projection-generated regular
*
-semigroups will be the main topic of our paper
[
29
]
.
This will include a general theory of presentations for maximal subgroups of arbitrary
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}(P)
, and detailed computations for
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}({\bf P}(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
, the free regular
*
-semigroups arising from the partition monoids.
These results will be compared and contrasted with known presentations
[
33
]
for maximal subgroups of the free (regular) idempotent-generated semigroups
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}(E)
and
\operatorname{\textup{{RIG}}}(E)
. This will again include explicit results for partition monoids, which will highlight the significant difference between
\operatorname{\textup{{IG}}}({\bf E}(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
and
\operatorname{\textup{{PG}}}({\bf P}(\mathcal{P}_{n}))
, and involve unexpected connections with
twisted
partition monoids
[
30
,
31
,
44
]
.
